THIS TURNS INTO SOMETHING
 
of a session: I finish the half bottle of Scotch and the whole bottle of ginger wine, which leaves me sluggish and foul-smelling by Monday morning. My eyelashes hurt, as if each had been snapped at the root.


My preparations for work on Tuesday complete (I
 
am
 
black bag, I say
 
nothing
, I react to
 
nothing
, I have many thoughts but they are merely the lubricating oil that sustains the furious motion of
 
nothing
), I have decided to visit my parents as soon as the painkillers kick in.


I go by three trains. I do this once a month when I’m not working, which is more often than not. Now that I have a steady job I ought to be able to greet them with a newfound confidence.


I would not, could not, subject my parents to the brutality of description. Who among us would come off well? Narrators are awful and they damage everyone they touch. So my parents will be represented by two pillars of rough-hewn stone, and that will have to do. I hang a garland of flowers around my mother and top up my father’s “eye well” (during the plague, coins were placed in a little hollow of water for sanitary reasons).




I walk Elliott,
 
my parents’ very badly behaved border collie puppy, who requires strenuous exercise of over six miles a day lest he become psychotic.


Come on, Elliott, I say. We’ll go and visit Bess’s grave.


What’s that to me?
 
says Elliott.
 
I acknowledge no predecessor. I piss on her grave and claim it as my own
.


Elliott, you brazen savage, you insolent mutt.


He gnaws on his lead as we leave by the kitchen door.


We do not routinely capitalize the names of dog breeds, I tell him, unless the name of the breed happens to be the name of a place. So when I write about you I will identify you as a border collie, small b, small c.


The Border is a place.


It’s a
 
notional
 
place. You’re neither Scottish nor English.


Damn sight more of both than you are
, he mutters, and pelts through a hedge.


I have to climb over the nearest gate and spend half an hour striding up and down the fields bellowing his name. I find him snapping at butterflies by the oxbow lake.

WHEN I REACH CAMPUS ON
 
the Tuesday of week two it is raining heavily and, even though I am dressed head to shin in an obelisk of reasonable-quality black leather, my feet are cold and the water is beginning to seep in through my eyeholes and trickle down the inside of black bag. I am willing to put up with this for the next hour and a half for the sake of the role, but I worry that I will be wet and squelchy in the lecture, and that the leather will smell potently, and that overall I will be more of a distraction than intended. The additional factors may even tarnish the experiment in some way.


Besides, I didn’t sleep well. I was anxious.


It seems best to find a quiet building where I can stand by a radiator and slowly dry off.


At the end of a path lined with silver birches a mildewed sign reads
 
DEPARTMENT OF MODERN LANGUAGES AND LITERATURE
 
and the oblong building ahead is engulfed in third-generation ivy. I bow before the silver disabled-access panel and press it with my forehead.


There is nobody at reception. A bright Patrick Heron abstract covers an entire wall; this was once an operation with a considerable art budget. There is a large beige heater against the left wall but it is surrounded by stacks of seminar chairs with miniature desk attachments on one arm. I climb two flights of stairs to the first floor. Here, where rain lashes the windows of the shallow balconies, I find an unencumbered radiator, billowing gouts of heat toward an open vent. I lean against a laminated sign that says
 
DO NOT MOVE
, which refers either to itself or to something that, nonetheless, has been moved. I close my eyes. A salaryman in Taipei might find that this reminded him of his restful commute, packed against other sorrowing bodies in a state of weightlessness. It might be
 
the closest he comes to genuine relaxation. I try to channel his gratitude as I drink the hot air.


I am drifting into a state of semiconsciousness when I feel the bag around the small of my back gather, as if caught in a mechanism, and I try to shake myself free, but the grip tightens and begins to pull me away from the radiator.


I am about to apologize to whom I imagine might be a warden or security guard when a woman’s voice says, Hey, it’s okay, and continues to drag me into the middle of the room, past the empty water cooler.


Oh.


Shhh.


I go limp and stop struggling, then turn to face her.


I recognize her as the woman who was glaring at me from the concrete bench last week. Justine. She smiles and lowers her gaze.


There is, I think, it being rare enough, something that passes between you, or emanates from you, when you are more than usually attracted to someone. Justine has high cheekbones, a slightly querulous turn to her lips, black and gray hair and the darkest blue eyes I have ever seen. She is wearing a black top with black trousers and a small black velvet jacket. She grabs a bunch of black bag at my waist and leads me through a pair of double doors, then halfway down a dimly lit hallway. She takes out a bunch of keys and flicks through them. A sign on her door reads
 
PROFESSOR JUSTINE PEARCE
.


Just … quiet, she says.


I realize I have been murmuring involuntarily.


She opens her door and pulls me in.


In her office she has an orange coffee machine with a jug, a large framed print of Hieronymus Bosch’s
 
The Garden of Earthly Delights
 
and a poster of a robot removing its face. The other three walls are floor-to-ceiling bookshelves but I can’t make out names or titles through my eyeholes. The room smells very strongly of nail varnish although as far as I can tell, Professor Pearce is not wearing nail varnish.


Do you want to sit?


I look at a row of three mismatched school chairs.


Do you speak?


Sometimes.


She seems relieved. Will you take a seat?


She sits down in an Eames office chair and turns it away from her desk. I take the middle seat, molded brown plastic with black rubber scratch caps on the end of the legs.


I hope you’ll forgive me for kidnapping you.


I think I saw you, I say, the other day.


Oh?


On a bench.


Ah yes.


I’m free until eleven, I tell her.


Professor Justine Pearce switches on her laptop and taps a few keys.


Several times over the last week I’ve seen you … faltering around campus. Makes a girl curious. And then, there you were, right on my landing. I’ll make some coffee?


She stands and flicks a red light on the machine.


I wouldn’t usually be this forward, she says.


I say nothing.


I was supposed to be giving a lecture on convergence but they changed the day and now it’s tomorrow. I’m used to giving it on Tuesdays; it’s confused the whole week for me. Do you know about convergence?


No.


It occurs somewhere between the fourth industrial revolution and total human extinction, she says. What are you, exactly?


I’m not supposed to talk about it, I say.


A student? Staff?


Staff.


Oh. She seems both reassured and disappointed, as if the mystery has been cheapened, as if this makes me both less and more of a problem. Or less short-term, more long-term.


I mean I’m sort of staff.


Yes, well.


Lab assistant. Temporary.


But you’re not supposed to talk about it?


It’s in my contract.


The thing about contracts, says Justine, is that you can break them with impunity as long as nobody involved in the contract finds out—they’re closed systems.


Hmm.


You can trust me, she says, wheeling over on her chair and putting a hand on black bag’s knee.


I don’t know you.


She smiles. I like travel, eating out and long walks in the rain. There. Now you know me. If you just tell me what you’re doing … I promise I have nothing to gain in telling anyone else. And they’re pretty lax on surveillance here. Although sometimes I realize I’m being monitored—that probably sounds rather paranoid, but it isn’t. Three years ago I had a fake student called Michaels.


Michaels.


I confronted him after class one day and never saw him again.


How did you know he was fake?


People can be terribly incurious, she says. I do not make a habit of picking up waifs and strays, but sometimes I see someone and I get a little signal, almost a physical feeling, an electric charge in the synapses: This person needs my help. And if I neglect that signal things tend to go badly. For me. Can’t ignore the signal.


Oh, I’m doing this of my own free will, don’t worry about that, I tell her.


Do you have a staff ID card?


They’re still printing it.


I’m interested in you, she says.


I’m flattered.


You’re hanging around my corridor in a bag. I get a little disconcerted, she says, regardless of the signal, by things that feel
 
off
. It really throws me, which is odd, because I generally have great self-possession.


I do not say anything, but Professor Justine Pearce
 
does
 
strike me as someone who has great self-possession.


There are certain things, and I won’t go into details, but there are some things that have happened to me that have given me a propensity for suspicion.


I sit forward in the chair. I’m not sure what it looks like.


My instructions are that I’m not supposed to say anything when I’m on campus, or at least that I’m to say as little as possible, I tell her.


She touches one of her ears nervously, then brushes her hair behind it. She looks directly at my eyeholes while I speak and I have the sensation of two other beings deep within each of us, saying hello to one another.


And who gave you these instructions?


Dr. Blend.


If she recognizes Dr. Blend’s name she doesn’t react.


Psychology, I add.


Oh, she says, smiling. Well I wouldn’t have heard of him. And this is some sort of study?


An experiment.


What kind of experiment?


I’m not entirely sure, I say. I’m an actor.


You don’t know what the experiment is?


I think it’s about how people react to me.


How
 
do
 
people react to you?


I tell her about the first lectures and seminars black bag has attended and that today will be the third, and that I have to sit still and not interact with anyone. I tell her about my journeys to and from campus.


She listens to me, curled up on the Eames chair with her hands braced around one of her knees. Sometimes she smiles grimly but doesn’t say anything.


Okay, she says. Okay. All of that sounds plausible. Maybe you’d like to come to my office on Tuesday mornings when the weather is this bad? Save you getting so bedraggled.


I’d like that, I say. Thank you.

I HAVE TO TAKE BLACK BAG
 
most of the way off to get to my keys and open my front door. I do this hurriedly so as not to be caught by my neighbors, and as I enter my narrow hall I’m met by a familiar potato-like smell.


The reasons for disliking a ground-floor flat are manifold and we needn’t go into them: the damp; the security issues; the disconcertingly irregular room shapes; the sense that this is what remained once the rest of the building was complete and carved up and they stuck a door with a Yale lock on the leftovers, an offcut, a cutaway; the proximity to the front door of the overall building; the traffic noise; the pedestrian noise; the staircase noise; the block’s many other denizens coming home drunk between 10 p.m. and 3 a.m. or leaving for work at 4 a.m. and making sure you know about it; the pantomime whispers and shushing meters away from your sleepless head; the fact that your bedroom, instead of a window, has disintegrating French double doors with a tarnished key rusted into the lock on the inside; the vaguely fungal smell; the almost total absence of natural light; the weedy little external square yard entirely choked with brambles for which you are responsible despite its utter lack of utility not to mention implements or anywhere to keep them; the fact that you are by far the most likely to be murdered; the hallway with plasterboard walls you bang your elbows on; the long, freezing kitchen with the tiny yellow bathroom at the other end of it so that all human functions merge into one somehow very saddening galley; the weird hum; the fact that you and you alone among dozens have to answer the front door to whatever nuisance chooses to assault the building’s serenity on any given evening; the way you are forced to care disproportionately and frequently about the wheelie bins—not for the lords and ladies in their sky castles, their lake dwellings, their stilt houses who discard such cares
 
like sins falling away from saints as they ascend above you—no, only for you, who live among bins; the pokiness; the dinginess; the honking taps; the trick or treats; the mornings, nights and afternoons; the chill draft and the lack of meaningful differentiation; that feeling of
 
lurking
; but I actually rather like it. Returning from Claudio’s penthouse still feels like coming home from a day at the office.




I sit in my chair
 
and count the pile of twenties I have accumulated so far. Three hundred and forty pounds for a few hours of sitting in a chair! That I am suddenly and unexpectedly wealthy ought to produce in me a sense of fiscal responsibility—I should squirrel it away, put it under my mattress like Francis Bacon in the hope it proliferates beyond any practical use.


Instead I wish to rebel against my erstwhile poverty and improve my environment.


I will remove the dreary, beige monstrosities from my disintegrating front bay windows and go to buy curtains I actually like. Me! Buying curtains! Imagine!


In a flash I understand why a newly successful musician might get fitted for golden, sparkling grillz, a personalized numberplate: to exercise some agency over the world that so insulted them.


I glare at the curtains I have endured for the last three years, mildewed death shrouds. I draw them and open them with a rasp, run my hand over their rough surfaces. A billowy patch of damp on the left, as if a former tenant pissed on it.


Fuck you, I whisper.


I pull my chair to the window, balance on it and tear the curtains from the eyelets, bundle them up and leave them by the door to shove in the wheelie bin. I give the sorry parcel a little kick for good measure.


I take measurements from ceiling to floor.


I dress smart-casually, put on my good shoes and visit the small, dark Curtain Emporium around the corner on the most nondescript
 
residential road in my neighborhood. An establishment that has never been relevant to me before and that I passed every day as if it were abandoned.


The stiff door activates an actual bell. The shop smells resinous, like a museum. It is dimly lit, but impeccably clean.


Their first customer in perhaps this century, I am treated like a king. After being shown many samples by a delightful little bald man in pince-nez, I choose some cotton herringbone weave pleats in “marshmallow” with blue embroidered details for £150. I pay in cash, naturally, which delights him.


Will sir need some help with installation?


No. Thank you, but I will take great pride and care in installing them myself, I tell him.




As I am hanging my beautiful
 
new curtains I feel as though I have seen them somewhere before. I shake it off. I replace each nasty plastic hook with a gleaming brass fastener. This accomplished, I step off my chair, move it back to its place, draw the curtains smoothly closed and admire my work.


The midday light filters through as if a team of technicians had perfected it. The entire room seems to sigh with satisfaction. I love them.


This is what I should have said in my audition. Before we start worrying about the environment we ought to get our soft furnishings sorted. Seek ye first the Kingdom of God.


They remind me of something good.


Off-white in just the right way. Trimmed with dark blue brocade of fleur-de-lis and vines … Ah: Samantha’s Spanish dress from the
 
Salumist of Avila
.


I run my hands down and around the curtains to smooth them and admire the weave, but it is as if my hand might alight accidentally on Samantha’s thigh.


Samantha, in that moment, strikes me as the pinnacle of femininity.


A man such as myself who can afford to choose and buy his own curtains must be in want of a woman to admire them.


Dearest, I text her, did you get your phone back?


I walk to the kitchenette to put the kettle on. I hum a few bars of “Master of the House.” The curtains look even better from the other side of the room and I can imagine Samantha standing by them, contemplating something.


Angel, so sweet of you to care! she replies. No. Insurance. I don’t like my new case very much.


But thank goodness you got to keep your number, I write, else I might have lost touch with you forever.


Don’t even say such things, she replies.


Shall we have dinner?


A few seconds elapse before Samantha replies, Are you serious?


I’m working! I tell her. And we could probably both use a square meal.


Samantha pauses again before replying.


Jam today.


Jam all day, every day! I text.




It is very important,
 
now that I am in love with Samantha, now that we will live in a riverside duplex and have three daughters named Calliope, Phoenix and Artemis who will all go to a Steiner school once I have retired from acting to consult on movies with psychological content so as to support Samantha as she auditions for a hard-hitting sex drama set in the ’70s, that I do not appear overeager.


I pull out her chair and then push it in again.


I sit down on the maroon velvet sofa side. Le Coucou is a traditional French restaurant recommended in the papers a couple of years ago, probably not what it was, but still basking in the afterglow. When I suggested it Samantha sent an embarrassed face emoji and then said
 
If you’re sure
?


Should a man take the chair side? To ward off potential aggressors? I’m not sure. I’d prefer to see her arrive, and in any case this side gives me a better view of the whole restaurant from which to ward off potential aggressors.


Samantha is wearing a bottle-green V-neck jersey dress that accentuates her broad and lovely shoulders. She has a large black leather handbag with a silver buckle on the strap.


Darling, you look a million dollars, I say, standing and almost knocking over my tall empty wine glass. I ordered you a negroni.


She raises her eyebrows, then leans in for me to kiss her on both cheeks.


Do you have any idea the last time I went out for dinner? she says.


I would expect you to be rebuffing suitors with a stick, I say.


I’ve forgotten how to do it. Babe, this isn’t a date, is it?


We sit down and she puts her handbag next to her like a pet.


A
 
date
? I say, adopting an American accent. You’re way out of my league.


Oh please, says Samantha.


This is just two old pros shooting the breeze, talking shop. We have to support each other, right?


In that case, darling. Samantha drinks half her negroni. She looks around. Remind me how this works?


They come to you and say,
 
Have you dined with us before?
 
And you say, no, but I’m assuming I tell you what I want to eat, you bring it to me and then I give you all my money. This is on me, by the way—it’s lovely of you to come at short notice.


I feel a little off, somehow, as if neither of us can quite function when we’re not backstage.


I finish my negroni.


As if we can only give each other what we require in short, pressured gasps. I ought to have suggested a quick hookup in a props cupboard. This is too deliberate, too stagey, a
 
date—
ha! How is anyone supposed to feel natural on a date?


I’m so tired, Samantha says, stretching. Split shifts in the café. And today this old man was
 
sick
—actually sick. Have you ever seen an old man throw up before? It was awful! I felt so bad for him. He wouldn’t stop apologizing.


Oh god, I say. You work in a café?


Well what do you do, babe? she says.


Between roles? Disintegrate.


I know you’re not the type to get your hands dirty.


It’s not so much that …


She reaches over and takes one of my hands, runs her fingers over it.


So soft, she says, like a prince.


Haha, shut up.


The waiter has cropped gray hair and a tight waistcoat over his white shirt.


Are we ready?


I wait until Samantha chooses the beef bourguignon and I order a bottle of red from the middle of the list.


I’ll have the same.


Minus the curtains I have one hundred and ninety pounds in cash remaining. If this overshoots I can just put this on my credit card and pay it off with next week’s wages.


So are you going to tell me about your big break? she says, gesturing at the restaurant. Do I have to guess?


It’s a bit weird, I say.


You’ve
 
not
 
become an escort? Her eyes widen as if she’s convinced this must be the case. She puts her hand to her mouth.


No. I don’t think I’m tall enough.


Honestly, she says, like that matters with your jawline and your big sad eyes.


I make the saddest expression I can.


Angelique said you must have become an escort.


Angelique?
 
I say, feeling a little dizzy.


I told her you were taking me out. She told me to watch myself. She laughs. I told her it was just two old pros shooting the breeze. She said,
 
God, if he can afford the Coucou all of a sudden, he must have become a sex worker.


I clear my throat.


Not that I judge, you know me. Her words.


Yes, I mean if I’d describe anyone as judgmental, I say.


Perhaps it is good that Samantha feels comfortable enough to drop Angelique’s name into conversation so casually, perhaps it indicates Angelique—my girlfriend for almost a year at the age of thirty-three—has ceased and desisted her character assassination of me, or that, as far as Samantha is concerned, all’s fair in love and war. But it is still the case that I have more or less excised Angelique from my memory and do not like to be reminded that she still exists or has any bearing on me whatsoever. I forgot they knew each other, but of course they do.


Oh angel, you look upset—I’m sorry.


God, no, it’s fine, I say.


I thought that was all such a long time ago, you know? Sweetheart.


It was, I say. It was. I’m totally fine—I haven’t thought about her in so long it’s just thrown me a little.


I briefly consider giving Samantha several twenty-pound notes toward the meal, striking the set and walking out.


I should change the subject.


Do you see her often, Angelique? I ask.


Here and there, says Samantha. She’s doing circus stuff again.


I make a mental note to avoid the circus and anything circus-adjacent at all costs.


That’s great! That’s so good that she’s doing that again.


Well, she’s very agile. Babe—genuinely, I didn’t mean to upset you; forget I mentioned her—let me just pop to the little girls’ room and we can start again.


Another take, I say.


Another take!


As she stands, Samantha gives my shoulder a conciliatory squeeze—an apology, a warning, a tacit agreement?—and goes in search of the bathroom.


For God’s sake. She looks so good.


A few more dates that I’m pretending aren’t dates and we could really have been something. But I cannot be part of any web that Angelique has had even an
 
auxiliary
 
role in spinning. I try to imagine what else she might have said about me. That Samantha should watch herself! Around me!


My heart is tapping out a distress signal; my face is hot and itchy. I’m sweating, but by the time it trickles down my neck, it is cold as the condensation on my negroni glass.


I reach under the table for Samantha’s handbag, drag it to my side and pop the clasp. You could fit a pumpkin in it. It’s perfect.


Once you overcome the initial feeling of transgression you can really do anything. I upend the handbag and pour its contents over the seat next to me, a little avalanche of pill packets, makeup, used train tickets, a turquoise paperback.


As if grappling with an oxygen mask I lift the handbag and pull it over my head.


The familiar smell of leather, right against my nostrils. I inhale deeply. I can’t see a thing, of course, but already I can feel my pulse stabilizing.


A presence approaches.


I forgot my— Oh.


I say nothing.


Darling, what are you doing? My stuff!


I shake my enclosed head.


Are you …?


Soon I will have to remove Samantha’s handbag from my head and help her put her possessions back into it.


Sweetheart, do you need me to call a doctor?


Soon. But for now her voice is distant and muffled, which makes me feel that I needn’t respond to anything at all.

20




I RETURN TO MY FLAT
 
vibrating with humiliation and pour myself half a tumbler of Whyte and Mackay. Sometimes I have vivid and detailed fantasies of putting a shotgun in my mouth, taking care to aim it right at the center of my brain, but this only applies to awful things I said or did years ago. Tonight’s wounds are too fresh for the thought experiment to have any effect.


Instead I take my drink into black bag, zip it up and sit on my chair in pleasant oblivion.


I was twenty-nine when I met Angelique in the cast of an experimental movement piece about Armenia. I wasn’t the strongest dancer and she took it upon herself to instruct me outside of official rehearsals.


Put your hands there. There. Stop being shy.


We got together after the opening night. Small attic theater above a pub. Somehow it drew a respectable crowd.


I mostly stayed at her place because mine was a dump.


I went through an unusual phase of getting a lot of auditions after that and I noticed that Angelique’s reaction to any work coming my way was to go quiet and barely acknowledge it.


Over the next six months it dawned on me that Angelique was possibly planning to kill me. Small signs I won’t go into. Nobody believed me at the time and I don’t expect you to either. But think about it: when you see a gloomy murderer’s mugshot in the newspapers, defiant, dead-eyed expression. The true face? The mask finally abandoned? No. Ultimately they’re as puzzled as you are.


It was an odd time. Angelique was publicly very affectionate if increasingly cool when we were alone in her flat. I was, of course, passive and accommodating, but I sensed a subterranean ocean of loathing, a sense that she wanted to punish me for something. Possibly for being passive and accommodating.


Anyway, it was so well hidden I could never even bring it up with her. I just anticipated. A wok to the back of the head while I read the LRB at the kitchen island. Poisonous mushrooms in a casserole. A setup with a deranged dealer.


What disturbed me the most—what did the most damage to my sense of self—is probably the fact that Angelique found me murderable at all; that there was something murderable about me. I hate that there’s something murderable about me.


That I’d probably help you plan the disposal of my body in advance if you asked nicely. I’d run to the shops.


You could say that’s just one person’s opinion, but it’s certainly one too many.


But thanks.


Eventually, in desperation, I’d take to openly saying
 
I think she’s planning to kill me
, and people would laugh.


Even Claudio maintains, to this day, that it’s possible I imagined it. You were smoking a
 
lot
 
of weed at the time, he’ll say, which wasn’t even true, which was in fact a rumor put about by Angelique.


Sudden bread knife frenzy, gently encouraged overdose.


I had to leave her no less than seven times, change my phone number and email address and cut several people out of my life before it stuck. Angelique took the breakup badly and started telling mutual acquaintances that I’d been very controlling, that I’d stopped her seeing her friends, that there were so many red flags she could see in retrospect, and that she was probably lucky to have escaped my clutches, although it would take some time for her broken heart to heal and to truly trust anyone again.


The thought of stopping anyone doing anything, let alone seeing their friends, is anathema to me. I said this to Claudio at the time, in desperation.


Claudio said that men do some pretty fucked-up things in relationships, so they’ve kind of poisoned the well, vis-à-vis trusting them and their accounts of things.


Yes, but I’m not like that. You’re not like that. You have to believe me.


Claudio sighed, probably calculating what this would cost him, socially, but knowing that ultimately he was going to take my side.


I don’t want you to take my
 
side
! I wailed. I don’t want there to
 
be
 
sides! I just want to be left alone!




Angelique did not, as it turns out,
 
have much to envy. For several months I was part of a five-strong, specifically non-comedic improv troupe. That was our whole thing: that it was improv, but not funny, preferably quite morose. We performed in a popular pub attic theater on Tuesday nights and developed a medium-sized loyal following. Therein I got scouted, which led to the highlights of my résumé: Gratiano in
 
The Merchant of Venice
 
(RSC, but an admittedly auxiliary part that didn’t lead to anything. “He talks a good deal of nothing”—a mot juste); Eric Birling in
 
An Inspector Calls
 
(regional, coastal, did not tour, possibly fell in the sea); Peter in
 
The Zoo Story
 
(university, studio theater) and an anonymous hectoring voice in something untitled (Royal Court). Or I’ve forgotten the title, I don’t know; it doesn’t seem plausible for a play not to have a title … During this time, I decided that I’d made it, but that was more or less the end of my run. I did land a role as Buttons in a regional pantomime last year. The star was a retired athletics commentator. I stayed in a very cheap B&B, the single occupant of a seven-bed room that felt oddly frightening to sleep in. The proprietor brought me a bowl of rice crispies on a tray every morning. With a spoon and a fork.


Times are hard.


The government hates us. The next will too.


I’m not complaining.


I can live within my means like a mollusk if I have to.


I know how lucky I am.


And I know I’m good.


Eventually you know you’re good.


Eventually you know you can be the one about whom they’ll say the material wasn’t great but the cast made it work.


You know you can make it work.


That’s all you’ve got. So you’d better know it.




I text Samantha.


Samantha, I’m so sorry. I don’t know what came over me.


I will pace up and down my living room and hallway until she replies, and if she doesn’t I will simply not go to bed and destroy myself with sleep deprivation.


My phone vibrates.


Already forgotten, sweetheart! she replies. I wish I could put my own head in my handbag sometimes.

21




DR. BLEND HAS WRITTEN
 
the word
 
Propinquity
 
on the whiteboard with a black marker.


You’ve read the Festinger, I hope, he says, in a voice that implies he suspects nobody has. Essentially, people who encounter one another more frequently tend to develop stronger relationships, he summarizes.


It feels so good to be in black bag. I nuzzle the inside of the leather a little. I would hate to be here as myself.


Who are you attracted to? says Dr. Blend. You, individually. Spend a moment thinking about that.
 
What’s your type
 
might be the crude way of putting it. Write it down.


I can see that the girl in the row in front of me writes
 
Tall men with mental health issues
 
in her jotter.


Dr. Blend talks a little about fan fiction and fan communities.


You make a space in your brain which other people can believe in.


They walk through the wet leaves thinking about it, he continues.


He says something about a study in an isolated Swedish town and for a while I think only about an isolated Swedish town, what I would do there and the life I would build for myself.


Swedish towns tend to feel bigger than they really are because they are very spread out. The population might be under a thousand and they’d be quietly interested in me. I’d walk in the forests, save up for a good coat with extraordinary insulation. I’d work in the sawmill, maybe. I’d be a bit wobbly at first, but my superiors would be supportive and gently amused as I learned the ropes. Good neighbors, welcoming the outsider, I’d get very drunk on oily spirits with them. It’s possible I’d get complacent in my work after a couple of months and sustain a life-changing injury—


But let’s refocus.


When Dr. Blend says this I realize I am at the point of falling asleep. I am growing accustomed to black bag—my aching legs, my self-consciousness, they mean nothing to me this morning. There are at least two hundred students in the room, gathered in clusters of four or five, with rows filled toward the back. Some of them glanced at me and conferred with each other at the beginning of the lecture. Since then I have been left alone. I am in a semi-comfortable seat at the end of the third row from the back. There are two empty seats beside me and then a girl with black hair who smells like shoe polish.


Dr. Blend writes down the lyrics to the Monkees song “Propinquity (I’ve Just Begun to Care)” on the whiteboard.


Birds who just happen, by chance, to be near each other. By an array of coincidences uncontrollable even by the most determined or manipulative methodologies. The problem here is that subliminal presentation confounds the presence of unconscious memory with the absence of conscious memory.


I cannot parse Blend’s sentence at all. I take myself back to the Swedish village. Since losing my right hand I have befriended an elderly woman named Birgitta. Someone once told me that nobody remembers anything other than the first and the last ten minutes of a lecture. It is not my job to listen anyway. Exactly, says Birgitta, boiling a pan for coffee. Ingen ko på isen.


There’s no cow on the ice.


At this point Dr. Blend has written:




C = p(‘old’{inclusion)—p(‘old’{exclusion)




next to the Monkees lyrics.


In the diffuse Swedish town I look out of Birgitta’s window, which, despite the cold, is just a wiry gauze giving out onto the pines.


Birgitta hums to herself in the kitchen—a traditional Swedish tune, lilting and melancholy. I am not sure why she has taken a liking to me. Perhaps only that I seem to need looking after.


Ultimately, we’re talking about avoiding the potential problems of previous research, says Blend.


I remain still as Dr. Blend plays a recording of a light faux-country song with a descending chord sequence that doesn’t quite work with the vocals, perhaps intentionally.


The students rise, gather their laptops or notebooks into their satchels and filter out, exchanging quiet observations.


Who’s your friend?


Haha. He’s in my seminar too, did I say?


No way?


Inside black bag I close my eyes.


You cannot try to see yourself the way others do, Birgitta says, putting a slab of marzipan on my tin plate. Isn’t that all we ever do? No, she says. Doing that will make you nuts. Worry gives a small thing a big shadow.

22




THE SECOND SOPHIE IS THE
 
first person to enter the seminar room after me. Dr. Blend is not here. I am sitting in my chosen space. She groans when she sees me and I make no sound or movement in response.


Sophie walks around the table and pulls out a chair. There are simply not enough points of reference within the room to tell you where she has chosen. No, wait, if I number the chairs clockwise with Dr. Blend’s slightly better chair at twelve, she takes a seat at four o’clock. This is on the opposite side of the room to the chair she sat in last week.


Sophie takes off her jacket, unwraps her gray scarf and takes a notebook and pen out of her shoulder bag.


She glares in my direction twice, then at her phone. Then she looks at me again.


Not a one-off, then?


I say nothing.


Not a little joke for the first class?


I try not to move or make any gesture that could be taken for a yes or a no.


I obviously don’t know who you are or anything about you, she says.


I tense my fingers and my toes.


I watch the second hand moving around the face of the clock.


Inside black bag I focus on very slightly tensing one finger at a time.


I’m not trying to be a bitch, she says. You probably think so. It’s just we pay a lot of money for this and you’re distracting and you’re unsettling, and you make some people really uncomfortable, so that’s obviously not acceptable, but even if that wasn’t the case— Oh hi, Briony.


Oh for fuck’s sake, says Briony, looking at me like I’m a spilled drink.


Right? says Sophie.


Briony snorts derisively.


We’ve drafted an email to Blend, says Sophie. I know you were busy.


Oh yeah, sorry.


You doing okay?


Yeah yeah.


The hubbub in the Blendless room increases as students enter, some saying, Oh, some not seeming to notice me or pretending not to care.


Dr. Blend does not appear until the entire class is present and seated, at four minutes past eleven. He is flustered and out of breath, his hair is fluffier than usual and his gray herringbone suit is crumpled. The air of a man who has just arrived from a protest or minor natural disaster. He picks his way through to the back of the room and slaps himself on the thighs.


I am terribly sorry to keep you all waiting.


Someone says, No biggie.


Dr. Blend pats himself down and takes out a pen.

23




HOW WAS YOUR DAY, BRO?


I don’t know, I say, unzipping and blinking in the harsh light. I don’t think anyone likes me.


On his main screen Claudio is playing a video game where you have to carry a baby through a war-torn city. The graphics are of a distressingly high specification. I watch as a dry explosion causes a dust cloud on the next block and Claudio’s protagonist crouches behind a burnt-out car, shielding the baby’s head.


You heard of this? he says. It’s
 
fucked
 
up.


He shuts it down, spins in his chair and points at me.


What you’re doing? That’s artistic integrity, that is, he says. Nobody likes what you’re doing but you’re totally committed to it.


He whistles in admiration.


You’re like the bizarro Claudio. After you die they’ll recognize you as the most innovative actor of your generation.


That’s a myth, I say, and a pernicious one.


There should be some way of getting you paid for it.


I
 
am
 
getting paid, I say, sitting down heavily on his sofa.


No, but I mean, says Claudio. You’re doing something cutting-edge here—taking this whole performance to the public. You’re giving it your all. I think you deserve more recognition.


I just want to do a good job, I say.


Exactly, says Claudio. I’m going to give this some thought.


Please don’t.


I’m already giving it some thought. Nothing you can do about it.


He turns in his chair again and writes something down on the notepad on his desk.


Okay, I’m knackered, he says. And I’ve got to file a piece on the war baby and umpire a tournament tomorrow. You okay just chilling? You can play anything you want.


I’m good, I say.


He smokes a joint, which I refuse, and then goes to bed.


I walk over to his desk to check the notepad and find that Claudio has simply written
 
black bag
 
against a single bullet point.




Next morning I watch the fiery
 
sunrise from Claudio’s giant window. I think of something blooming in a petri dish.


I check to make sure Claudio is breathing and then I collect my toothbrush and quietly close the door of his bedroom by pulling the matte chrome door handle down very slowly and then keeping my hand on it as it rises again. I go to his fridge and I take a can of iced coffee and an individually packaged custard tart. I consume this quickly at the black marble counter and then I blow my nose on a piece of kitchen towel.


I watch the lights blinking on several of Claudio’s consoles and suppose that he is downloading or uploading something. When it uploads it will be subject to thousands of comments from obsequious followers, irrepressible pedants, juvenile psychopaths. What is
 
up
, guys. Hit the like button, ring that bell, let’s try to get to 500,000. Sometimes I worry that the whole thing is causing Claudio non-superficial and lasting soul damage.


But god, whose job isn’t?


We start to resemble what is asked of us, I imagine Dr. Blend saying. The trouble is none of us can truly say what that is.


I try to cram black bag into my rucksack but it’s too big.


I unfold and look at black bag. I hold it at arm’s length in front of me.


I climb into it and crouch to do up the zip.




I arrive back at my place
 
with chafed feet, eager to get out of black bag, or to assume the larger black bag of my apartment. I will spend three
 
days here, dedicated to research, reading for pleasure, doing calisthenics and maybe drinking a bottle of wine, and then return to Claudio’s flat on Monday night for the working week to begin again. I can feel a cold coming on so I should take it easy. Acting is a dictatorship of the healthy. If you’re going to land a part in a long-running musical you’d better be preternaturally
 
well
.


I admire my new curtains. As I acquire more wealth I will set about making this room my own: an old wooden globe, a dark and pretty drinks cabinet with a complicated mosaic inlay. A mahogany bookcase. A tall, thriving bird-of-paradise houseplant.


All of these will go with my record player. I put on Keith Jarrett’s
 
The Köln Concert
. I turn on the standard lamp with the William Morris shade. If there’s one thing worth spending on, one thing that can truly make or break your quality of life, it’s lighting. I pour myself a brandy. I sit in my green leather armchair and I pick up
 
The Collected Poems of Frank O’Hara
. I have a television. Sometimes I like to tell people that, in the way other people like to tell people they don’t have a television.

24




THE KIND OF RAIN
 
that only happens on a Tuesday, heavy like preordained rain, rain augured and written about by prophets eons ago. This will be a wet place.


I think if we’d focused our warnings about climate change on the fact that, for the United Kingdom alone, it would result not in the country becoming even
 
half a degree warmer
 
than a cold day in November, but rather a slow and relentless year-round icy drizzle into oblivion, we’d have got a lot more people on our side.


That’s what I should have said in that audition.




The moment I get to campus
 
I head directly to the Department of Modern Languages and Literature. It looks as though someone has polished the sign, although nobody has gardened and the building is still smothered in ivy.


I climb the stairs to the first floor and I go through the double doors marked
 
ENGLISH
 
and I walk down the long corridor until I come to 112, Professor Justine Pearce’s office.


I knock.


Nothing.


I bow my head. Perhaps I will find a chair in a communal area.


I feel a firm but gentle hand on my shoulder.


I already have strange expectations of Professor Justine Pearce so I am disappointed when she says something as functional as
 
I’m so sorry—I’m double-booking everything this morning.


She unlocks her door.


Please come in.


Hi.


I’m
 
really
 
glad you came, she says, taking off her coat and hanging it on a peg mounted on the back of her door. I saw the rain this morning and I thought, I wonder. And I’m so sorry I was late. Do sit. I thought, I wonder if he’s someone who takes people at their word.


I was grateful for the offer.


Which is a rarer quality than you think. How is your work?


I tell her that students have complained.


I suppose they would. But I suppose that’s data.


It’s all data, I agree.


But are you happy?


Am I
 
happy
?


You’re being looked after?


I feel … I shuffle inside black bag. I feel a great sense of tranquility.


By being made invisible?


I don’t know.


Not that you’re particularly invisible.


She looks at my feet.


I can infer that you’re Caucasian and possibly male.


That’s usually enough to get a fair idea of someone.


Ha.


We’re quite generic.


You contain multitudes, I’m sure. Something that interests me—Justine unzips one of her boots and adjusts her black tights—is whether it might be possible to undertake a relationship with someone who has foregone much of their identity.


A friendship?


No, not really.


Professor—


Christ, she says, that’s adorable, but you can call me Justine.


Justine.


Yes. Are you straight?


Bi.


Well that’s a coincidence, she says. I’m not making you uncomfortable, am I?


Not at all.


Good. And you’re okay with me? Doesn’t feel like breaking character?


I think about this. It hadn’t occurred to me not to, I say. The rules only apply when I’m in class. But I’m not supposed to talk at all. I haven’t talked to anyone else.


So this is just for
 
me
? she says, pleased.


I love when women seem to be happy with me. It makes me feel like the entire world is in balance.


Private show, I say.


Good, says Justine. Let’s keep it that way. A little about me: BA Oxon, PhD Stanford, although I don’t like to go on about it; my thesis was on instrumentality—you can look it up.


I will.


I’ve been here for ten years and part of me absolutely fucking hates it, but they treat me adequately and my modules are popular. Sometimes—she gestures at the window—the evening light comes through that tree
 
just so
 
and I feel lucky to be here.


I am quickly developing a crush on Professor Justine Pearce.


I love that, I say.


It’s not unusual for a professor’s work to have an impact outside of academia, she says. In fact it’s something institutions are very much in favor of. Play that right and they barely ask you to do anything else. Now with my discipline there are some clear crossovers with the tech industry, which happens to be obscenely affluent as well as prone to suggestion. At a certain point you start to ask yourself why you work in a literature department,
 
English
 
literature, of all things, but we can circle back to that.


Right.


Naturally you can find your research drifting toward … answering the demands of the industry to which you’re adjacent.


Oh, I know all about that, I say.


There are some very damaged men in technology, of all ages, says Justine, and, I know, it’s hard to picture a smaller violin—but when damaged men have money and influence, that’s a problem for the whole world. It can also be a problem for their companies and stakeholders,
 
which seems to be the more urgent concern if the commissions are anything to go by.


You work with them? You’re like a kind of therapist?


I observe them.


She looks out the window.


They’re
 
men
, she says. Caught between thousands of years of abusing their power and a reckoning they bitterly resent and at the same time feel they fully deserve. Makes them a bit fucked up.


It does make us a bit fucked up, I say.


In the experimental method we remove certain variables and observe the results, Justine says.


She looks at me again.


Maybe it’s not about your lack of identity, she says. Maybe it’s actually quite the opposite. Maybe it’s something about
 
you
.


Me?


Something about the kind of person who would … even be capable of … what you’re doing.


Professor Justine Pearce places her black boots by the door and walks barefoot to her desk, where she picks up a wooden toy snake painted orange and white, made of triangular segments that can be bent and rearranged into a variety of patterns. This she does.


Will you have a coffee?


For sure.


What I’m suggesting is a little side hustle, if you have time.


I’ve got nothing but time.


An experimental relationship.


Relationship?


With me, she says. It wouldn’t impinge on your obligations with doctor whatever his name is.


Blend.


She snorts. Psychology. Ha! What a discipline. What is there left to understand about us? We’re done. Done!


What would I have to—


Well, what do you usually do?


I’m sorry?


If you’re asking someone out.


I adjust my eyeholes so I can see Justine properly. Maybe this is the answer. I’ve spent so long dating in the same gene pool of frustrated would-be performers, artists with the same neuroses, the same dubious talents and resentments, the same poverty and insecurity and bloody-minded self-belief, the same martyrdom to a craft that does not seem to love us back. Maybe a striking older woman with an established position in the world and a refined area of expertise, a woman for whom I am already and always will be black bag—and therefore have nothing to hide, nor anything to reveal—can open me up to a completely different way of life!


Are you free Friday? I ask.


Friday’s no good, sorry.


Saturday?


Actually now that I think of it I’m absurdly busy at the moment, Justine says. Sorry. We might need to postpone for a couple of weeks. Do you have anywhere in mind?


No …


Of course you don’t. It would need to be somewhere fairly dark where you wouldn’t draw too much attention or feel self-conscious.


That’s what I tend to look for anyway.


Yes.


Black bag or no.


So what this means is somewhere very expensive, she says.


Right.


She tries to hand me a cup of coffee.


How do you—


If you could put it on the floor.


Of course.


I step over the beige coffee cup and undo the zip all the way down. The steam rises up the side of my body. Trying not to show Justine my arms, I take the coffee cup, pass it from my left to my right hand and very carefully do up the zip again. I sit down carefully.


Excellent, she says. Could you give me your phone and unlock it?


Why?


I need to be able to contact you, but a woman in my position can’t just go around handing out her contact details.


Sure.


As I’ve learned to my cost many times.


I shake my phone loose and drop it on the floor.


Thank you. She picks it up. I’m downloading One-Way.


What’s One-Way?


It’s just a messaging app. I can text or call you, and you can reply, once, to the previous message, but you can never instigate communication.


Sounds like my—


So it only works one way. I’m sorry—it’s nothing personal, but I have to have strict boundaries.


No, it’s fine, you don’t even know me.


And I hope I never will! she says, beaming, as if she’s been waiting for someone she will never know her whole life.


Justine finishes with my phone and places it on the floor beside me.


We sit in silence for a moment.


Justine?


Yes.


Dr. Blend is a doctor, but I think he’s quite old.


Oh?


And he’s not a professor.


Hmm. No, he’s probably quite bitter about it. Maybe you’re his ticket to a professorship.


Are you quite young for a professor?


Oh, bless you, she says. No, I’m forty-seven. I’m just attractive and very good at my job.

MONDAY NIGHT I RUB OINTMENT
 
onto my feet in the merciless glare of Claudio’s spotlights.


You’ve got to look after those, dude.


I want them to toughen up.


Yeah, tenderfoot life.


So maybe I need to find the opposite of a moisturizer.


Claudio works on his phone for thirty seconds.


Ten percent tannic acid tea soak, he says.
 
But
 
you still want to moisturize. Especially if there are cracks.


Noted.


I’ll order some foot tea.


You don’t have to.


For you, man, anything. It’ll be here next week. Hoo-eee. I’m still a bit scattered. Still a bit scattered from two days ago.


Why, Claudio?


Don’t worry so.


You
 
are
 
a worry.


Just some complimentary shit from Stutters. These, like, little pips. Nasty experience. I told him so. It was worse than Monkey LSD.


Monkey LSD?


Not “Monkey”: 1P-LSD,
 
one-p
. An analog and functional derivative of LSD.


You need to enunciate, Claudio.


We didn’t all go to public school.


We went to the same school!


Yeah, but you carry yourself differently. Anyway, those little pips. I felt like I was swinging in a hammock made of beams of light, but the lights were horrible to look at. Like pain, but not.


You didn’t call me.


I couldn’t work out how to charge my phone.


Ah.


It turns out that the complimentary shit Claudio was given by Stutters was Hawaiian baby woodrose seeds. He gives one to me. A little nib. It looks, in all honesty, exactly what I would expect something named “Monkey LSD” to look like, mishearing notwithstanding. The sort of thing monkeys might chew a few of to escape the agony of monkey selfhood.


You have to take at least a dozen. So that won’t do nothin. So go ahead.


I put the little Hawaiian baby woodrose seed in my mouth.


Monkey LSD.


As I chew it tastes of varnish and vinegar and peat and imitation sugar, and then, the way a fine wine suddenly reveals its depth and detail, it tastes like shit; like actual shit; it tastes categorically demonic, the way I’ve always imagined a poison to taste when I’ve had to perform the part of someone who’s been poisoned. I start to gag.


Oh man, says Claudio.


Water.


Yeah yeah, coming right up, man.


Fucking Stutters.


I go to Claudio’s extremely bright bathroom and vomit up what feels like a thick woolen glove.


Sorry.


I brush myself down.


Don’t apologize.


You’ve never really told me why you won’t partake. Aside from your evidently weak constitution.


I haven’t?


You won’t even share a joint. I mean I can admire that. But why not?


Out of respect for God.


Ah.


At the age of twenty-two, and in aid of my career, I prayed and I asked God to allow me to experience a fraction … a thousandth—He
 
said no—a ten-thousandth—still He demurred—a millionth—here He acceded—of human suffering. And in a second, a fraction of a second, a millionth of a second, the walls of my mind were razed to their foundations and I’ve never fully recovered.


So you don’t fuck with the doors of perception.


I don’t.


Too much respect for God.


Do you have a beer?


This is all relevant to black bag, says Claudio, writing on his notepad again. All of this is usable.


Claudio fetches me a rhubarb sour from a brewery called Honest Engine.

26




ALL OKAY?


Just great, I say, brightly.


At this point, whenever he requires to see me, I have taken to presenting myself to Dr. Blend as black bag. Already—this is perhaps quite funny—I have come to see my body and my face as internal organs. There is no reason to show Dr. Blend my internal organs.


Are
 
you
 
okay though?


Why do you ask?


I decide not to mention Dr. Blend’s harassed appearance in the previous week’s seminar. He looks composed this morning.


They mentioned a complaint.


A complaint?


The students. A group email.


Oh yes, well, that’s to be expected.


It’s possible that Dr. Blend does not mean to discuss the experiment with me. It’s possible that my trying to enter into dialogue about the experiment with him is an irritation. I have been employed to perform a specific role and my personality is irrelevant, a distraction, or something I should keep to myself along with my internal organs. I ought to bear this in mind.


I also feel a little guilty for telling Professor Justine Pearce about the experiment. Maybe it was avoidable. Even if it is unlikely that she would in some way try to sabotage the experiment. I mean what do I know? Maybe academics can be as bad as actors.

ON MY WAY TO CAMPUS
 
I call in on a newsagent.


My good man, I say, ostentatiously, before the clerk has had a chance to perceive any incongruity. As you can see I am incapacitated. Would you be so kind as to fetch and scan a copy of the

, which I will then pay for contactlessly with an internal bank card? And then if you would nudge it off the counter and onto the floor. Thank you. Much obliged.


This is either an act I can get away with once, or I can establish a pattern and eventually I wouldn’t even need to say anything; just nudge my way into the shop and the proprietor, familiar with me by this point, would do the rest.


Within black bag I carefully double fold and tear around the day’s cryptic crossword and slide it into the gaffer tape frame I have made to size.


I am not sure what to do with the rest of the newspaper. Due to my decision to trial being naked inside black bag I cannot take it off to put it in a bin. And I do not like to litter.


I tuck the paper under my arm where I feel it begin to absorb my own sweat.


Two women laugh at me as I pass the cinema, as if aware of my predicament.


Good morning, I say, and they laugh again as they walk past.


Once I am on campus I will not speak at all. The only exceptions are Dr. Blend’s office and Professor Justine Pearce’s office. But it occurs to me that maybe I should never speak, as long as I am black bag. It feels, on some critical level, disrespectful. There ought to be some solution to this.




A quirk of the ancient monastic calendar
 
followed by academia and education more broadly is that the vast majority of an institution’s
 
activities must take place in cold, in wet, in darkness: we begin in October and plunge into a winter that never really ends until the final seminar in March, last of the late gray slush melting; tentative buds, go revise and we’ll see you next October when you’re dead again.


I have always disliked the awkward way academic years must be recorded, ’22/’23, ’23/’24, ’24/’25, ’25/’26, ’26/’27, ’27/’28, ’28/’29, the years edging and overlapping each other like lumpy starfish in tide pools, in grotesque colors, in time-lapse. It unsettles me.


I took one term in English literature at the University of Durham, wrote a single 4,000-word paper on chivalry, then dropped out. I went into a bit of a funk. Stage school was
 
forbiddingly
 
expensive in the ’90s, not like today when
 
absolutely everything
 
is forbiddingly expensive, thus bringing about a kind of negative equality. It doesn’t seem worth being parsimonious in one arena only for another—a new fan belt, a winter utility bill—to fuck you into the sky.
 
Bring on the big tellies, the fine dining, the personal trainers, for tomorrow we die.
 
It was a big deal then, for my parents—being two pillars of rock and understanding little outside of stones, stillness and vistas—to support me. I must have been convincingly depressed. They remortgaged their house—such is the privilege of the only child—and, with a monologue from
 
Top Girls
, I was offered a place at East 15 and started the following autumn.

I pick up a wooden toothbrush and scan around for toothpaste until I notice a little black glass caviar jar marked
 
Oral Care
. The unguent inside is jet-black
 
and is applied to the brush with a small wooden teardrop attached to the side of the jar on a length of twine.


It tastes like the sea at night.


My preference is for personal grooming products that look and smell like powerful toilet cleaners, but I am willing to concede, as I pick a literal piece of
 
heather
 
out of my eye after washing my face, that I have been following the wrong metaphors. Or that I could learn to love such things as Justine’s ethical, sustainable, cruelty-free and almost completely ineffective washing-up liquid, toilet sands, laundry pebbles, because she does.

CLAUDIO, WHERE HAVE YOU BEEN?


Ah, says Claudio. Sorry, yeah. Some business in town and I forgot what day it was.


The room is dominated by a giant electric-pink space hopper with an unpleasant gurning face printed on it in black. It must have a diameter of at least six feet—would be impossible to mount, and must have been inflated after delivery—and is very ugly. I vaguely recognize the face from a meme.


I do not ask where it came from or who sent it because Claudio is constantly sent all manner of things.


I look at Claudio’s arms.


Have you been working out? I ask him.


Pshaw, he says.


I sit on the floor next to the outsized space hopper and inhale the rich, sweet smell of the glossy rubber.


You seem sad, bro.


It’s the crushing.


This again, says Claudio. The crushing again. You’ve got a project, you’ve got a focus.


That’s something, I say, sadly. Did you know that women cover up the truth about mortality?


Why would they do that?


They have to. Men could do it, if they really put their minds to it, but it doesn’t come naturally. I don’t know if it’s mostly cultural. My worry is there’s no culture at all.


Oh, darling, says Claudio.


That we’ll talk and talk to nobody, that we’ll take our art very seriously but nobody else will.


Is it the space hopper? Do you want me to move it? Do you want to throw it off the balcony?


No, I want to stare at it.


There’s always been trash, says Claudio. You can either ignore it or become an expert in trash.


You’re not going to tell me where you were?


He goes to his kitchen counter, draws a glass of water and stirs two fizzing tablets into it.


I’m building something, he says. And you said you didn’t want to know.


No, I say, okay.


All you have to do is keep being black bag.


He takes a sip of his concoction and starts to giggle.


What is it?


We’re going to be rich, he says.


You’ve been selling the … stakes?


Oh, they went within the week, he says. Black bag is currently worth one hundred and fifty grand.


Oh my god.


And that’s with an introductory offer. Our shares are probably going to double within a month.


But how? I say. How do you even … For what?


I don’t know what you’re doing, he says.


I don’t know what
 
you’re
 
doing.


But keep doing it, says Claudio.

reacts when they see me in the seminar room. Their engagement is still a little hostile, but more curious.

DO WE GET TO HAVE
 
THIS MONEY?
 
Can I go and buy my lunch with it now?


No, says Claudio. It’s not like that.


He closes his laptop: the contemporary moment’s gesture of generosity nonpareil.


So what even is it? I say, stepping out of black bag and shielding my eyes from his halogens. Is it like the “idea” of money?


Well I’m not going to cash out
 
now
! Claudio says. That would be completely insane.


I suppose what I’m asking, I say, is do we
 
ever
 
get to cash out?


Of course we do, says Claudio. We find some investors, we drive engagement, we make it as good as it possibly can be and then someone else buys it all and runs it into the ground. Claudio performs a dive with his hand and whistles a descending note.


He pulls his laptop open again and clicks on a file. He shows me five MS Paint drawings of a black rectangle with googly eyes.


These are the first NFTs, he says.


You know I don’t understand anything about that, I say.


We’ve been through this so many times! says Claudio, exasperated. When you—you personally—buy a book you’re not buying a small quantity of paper and ink, right?


Sure.


Well it’s the same thing. You’re buying the aura of something, you’re investing in someone’s ideas and expression, whatever the hell that means. You’re buying something
 
intangible
. The cost price of a canvas and some paint is a few quid.


The cost price of a doodle you did on your computer is zero.


The former is
 
negligible enough to be zero
, says Claudio. Don’t be obtuse.


The idea is people buy the doodles because they’re interested in the
 
idea

They’ve seen you wandering around, or they’ve seen footage of it, or they’ve heard other people talking about it, says Claudio. They don’t know what it is but they want in. What for?


I’m working on that, says Claudio. But it has limitless possibilities.


A flaneur, I say.


Whatever.


Exactly. My only concern is this cheapens the whole affair.


It does the exact opposite!


Cheapens the artistic statement, I mean.


And what is that? What’s the objet d’art?


That’s not what objet d’art means.


What’s the objet d’art for
 
you

I don’t think it’s for me to say, I say.

I’m a pragmatist, says Claudio.


Right.


I don’t want to do this forever, he says, waving a dismissive hand at his arrangement of consoles and cameras. Too many people feel like they
 
own
