I AM WEARING A RED,
 
white and black plaid shirt tucked into faded blue jeans. My one good brown belt. In the past few days I have not taken black bag off at all, even to sleep, so it feels strange going out into the world in this ensemble: I have a febrile vibration in my chest, as if the prelude to a bad cold. I duck back into my ground-floor flat assuming I’ve forgotten something, but I haven’t.


In the lobby I sort out the other residents’ mail into neat piles on the Formica sideboard and emerge from the front door, blinking as if leaving a matinee.


On the street I find that I expect people to stare at me, and feel unsettled when they don’t. I expect hesitation and subtle double takes, the energy of weighing up whether to engage, to fear and avoid or to mock. In its place, nothing. A glance as long as a microscopic calculation, whatever we ask of a fairly harmless-looking stranger.


I mess up my hair a little bit.


In taking time out from black bag I feel as though I am cheating on myself, lying about who and what I really am. In going to see my parents while playing the part of an ordinary young white man in a plaid shirt, I feel disingenuous.


A pigeon is pecking a piece of pastry on the pavement and doesn’t move as I approach. I step into the gutter to pass it.


But is this not the way it always is, seeing one’s parents? We do not really want them to know or see how we go about in the world, what strange compromises and strategies we have developed, our laziness and vigor, charm and petulance. We’d be embarrassed, so would they. Better for all concerned for them to think whatever they want, and to receive us intermittently as a little child who worries precociously for them.


They do, by the way, give me cause for worry.


On the other hand I am very relaxed with my parents and feel no pressure to be interesting at all. Once my mother asked me how I was doing and I told her that I had, that day, received a new debit card, just in time for the impending expiry date on my current card; but that I hadn’t signed the back of it yet, so technically it wasn’t valid.
 
That’s good
, she said.
 
Better sign it before the old one runs out
. Oh, I will, I said.


That was a little too close to characterization. From now on they will be two stone pillars again and that’s all you’re getting.


Furthermore, my parents ought to be protected, I think, from black bag as much as from narrative description.


A prophet is not without honor save in his own country. We tend to pity the prophet in that equation.


But think of their
 
families
.


I experiment by smiling at a woman in a bomber jacket pushing a beige pram with impressive suspension. She ignores me.


Having a face that can convey things. Wow.


Do we not so often get caught with a sour look that we didn’t even mean, something non-topical, not so much a slip of the mask as a universal pity?


Perhaps I owe myself a little time out of black bag. I need to remember that it is a job.


I have always had this problem when I feel particularly well suited to a part.


I have always taken parts to which I feel particularly well suited.




At the station I realize that
 
I can get a coffee, maybe even a leathery croissant, without any encumbrance and sit on a bench and simply consume them. My first paycheck from the university is yet to clear, but perhaps Claudio will wire me some money from my 20 percent of his franchising scheme. Perhaps I ought to be more parsimonious; goodness knows how long his efforts will carry on, and a responsible man would open a savings account.


But why should I deny myself the simple pleasure of a coffee and a pastry? Enjoy having limbs and space.


Something happens when I get to the coffee concession in the waiting room on platform 3.


I hear a low, close mosquito whine behind my shoulder again and I turn to see a little black
 
X
-shaped craft, no bigger than a tennis ball, pivot to the left and lift into the sky.


I realize that I am at the front of the queue.


Yes?


I’d like a …


The barista smiles patiently.


I’m sorry. I’d like a …


I massage my jaw.


A coffee? she suggests.


Yes. I’m sorry.


A normal coffee?


I’d …


It is as though something has sealed my throat, as if it had never been a throat at all. We are made of such tiny, delicate parts—why should they ever work?


I cough and try again.


I …


It occurs to me that I am no longer fully myself. That I have lost the knack of performing myself. A snail without a shell, a sucked lozenge. I lack the selfhood to even say what I want.


A cappuccino?


I nod vigorously.


Although who ever really says what they want?


Anything else?


Yes.


Something to eat?


She picks up a pair of tongs and hovers over the glass plates on the counter.


I’d like a …


A cake?


I shake my head.


A croissant?


I nod.


There you go, she says, pleasantly, folds the croissant into a brown paper bag and turns to work the coffee machine.


Thank you, I say, the gift of speech miraculously returning to me the moment I no longer need it. Thank you so much.




God, what a pig’s ear
 
I’ve always made of even the simplest interactions, how false my smile and weak my voice. On the train I have to walk through two busy carriages to find one that is almost empty. I take a seat and fold down the tray, which creaks like a pterodactyl and stops at a downward slope on which my coffee won’t balance. I sit down with my rucksack still on my back and I twist my neck trying to remove it with one hand while I hold my coffee in the other, my croissant on the seat next to me where at any moment a stranger might sit on it and squash it flat.


Would I still eat it, if that happened?


I scowl at the busted tray and flap it up and down, once.


We’re really supposed to
 
achieve things
 
in our lives when doing literally anything is this much of a ball-ache?


Just as I have freed both shoulders with no little discomfort, the train begins to move and I involuntarily pour a sluice of coffee onto my shoe.


The coffee is too hot but I drink it quickly and then put the almost empty cup on the floor where I hold it in place with my foot.


I text Claudio to ask if he’s okay. Hope you’re okay, I write.


It’s kicking off! he replies. Come round asap.


A very young child twists in his mother’s arms, inconsolable,
 
I want daddy. I want my daddy. I want to go back to daddy.


This, I think, is difficult for all of us to hear in different ways.


I eat the croissant, which I had temporarily forgotten about. It is an effort to swallow.


Then I start to play a round of solitaire on my phone. Solitaire is one of a small category of games that is far superior in its digital form than it is in real life. The last time I tried to play it with an actual deck of cards, it took up most of a table and I couldn’t even reach the farthest stack of cards without getting up from my chair.


I finish a hand in 146 moves then look up and see a woman cramming a suitcase into the overhead luggage storage, taking off a long houndstooth coat and then putting it back on again. When she turns to face me I realize that it is Justine and I grin at her before realizing there is no way she could possibly recognize me. I look back at my phone and grin again, as if to myself, to make clear that I am just someone who grins apropos of nothing and not to be paid any mind, then I scowl, to make it clear that I do not grin constantly and was not grinning at her, then a little grin again, then neutral.


I have not had to think about facial expressions in public for some time and it is, frankly, rather demanding.


The woman I have no right to know as Justine frowns, pulls her checkered wheelie suitcase out of the luggage storage. It looks heavy and she makes a small sound of exasperation as she lowers it a little too fast to the floor. I could offer to help. No. That feels inappropriate.


I’d know who she was, she wouldn’t know who I was.


Having the upper hand in any situation is morally reprehensible.


Justine unzips the suitcase and takes out a book called
 
The Tetrapharmakos
 
and a pencil. Then she puts the suitcase directly onto a chair at a seat with a table and sits down next to it. She glances around irritably.


There is something childlike to her essentially unobserved and private petulance, which only serves to emphasize, for me, her poise and sophistication. I want to take her feet in my lap and unfasten her shoes and rub her feet.


Justine sighs and opens the book and puts the pencil in her mouth.


It is quite possible that she is on her way to the Lake District.


I would like to speak to her, but it seems inappropriate. More than that I would like to go and sit next to her and lay my head on her shoulder.


Instead I occasionally glance at her reading, during which she looks angry and beautiful. Occasionally she looks out the window and I pretend that I am looking out of the same window rather than at her face in profile and the reflection of her face in profile. My breath feels weighted and almost tearful.


It is a great pleasure, close to anguish, to see your lover as someone else sees them. For your lover to be admired by someone else is perhaps the greatest pleasure.


I do not know why that is … Whether it comes from something shameful and primitive. I possess something you want. Therefore I am superior to you.


If Justine were to look longingly at me, at
 
this
, would that be disloyal to black bag?


I move the two of clubs onto the three of hearts.


I can itemize my significant past relationships (of course Angelique, most recent and catastrophic) alongside my previous roles, only in this case it was always the same role.


At some point it doesn’t seem to matter whether one embraces the traditional precepts of masculinity or not; it’s more what I call the Compensatory Model, which is itself not unlike a game of solitaire. So, (a) I may not be physically strong or visibly muscular; (b) I don’t have any money to speak of, am, in fact, flat broke; (c) given the above I often lack self-esteem and the necessary level of confidence required to function in society; (d) I may also, in fact, be terminally indecisive and so worried about upsetting anyone—given the lowliness of my station—that I cannot even commit to or venture an opinion on the choice of pub on an evening out; what if we went there and it was bad? Therefore (a) I could never realistically protect you or our offspring if push came to shove; (b) I can neither provide for you nor buy you thoughtful gifts nor take you on holiday nor project forward into a stable future together; (c) I will often be quite fretful and insecure; (d) so that’s annoying, isn’t it?


For inadequacies (a) through (d) there must be some form of reimbursement; supposedly I would bring in their place (a) intelligence, (b) kindness, (c) good humor and (d) compassion. But dear lord, what lofty qualities—not to mention transitory and ambiguous. What even
 
are
 
they and who among us can manifest them constantly? This is really asking one’s personality to do a lot of the heavy lifting, and a personality is somewhat like the weather. What it came down to is that I was offering, in place of the traditional qualities of masculinity, precisely nothing. A vortex that could consume the two of us. Eventually, sometimes after months or years of unhappiness, my girlfriend would begin to feel that I was
 
not a real man
; but this would be as painful for her to admit to herself as for me to infer it from her gradual withdrawal of affection.


The truth is nobody wants a sensitive man at all. A sensitive man may be very kind and obliging, largely because he has a delicate psyche to protect and has learned that the best way to do this is to avoid conflict altogether. A sensitive man, contra the First Epistle to the Corinthians, keeps a
 
comprehensive
 
record of wrongs, remembers every moment you hurt his feelings in intricate detail so as to avoid taking similar damage in future. The only cure for this is vigorous, mutually ecstatic sex—the only context in which a sensitive man truly escapes or transfigures himself—but by this point you’ve become so weary of the sensitive man’s complex feelings and mopey evasions you’d just as soon have vigorous, mutually ecstatic sex with a boiled potato.


I move the ten of spades onto the jack of diamonds.


Whenever she stops reading, Justine looks preoccupied and troubled by something.


But don’t we all.


From the age of seventeen to thirty-two all of my relationships ended the same way. We’d be so disappointed in each other it was difficult to be in the same room; she’d be generally so displeased to see me I’d go into paroxysms of intelligence, kindness, good humor and compassion in an attempt to prove my worth; but the intelligence would seem fussy, the kindness submissive, the good humor obsequious and the compassion feeble. Eventually, at her behest, we’d need to talk and, like an employee
 
before a severe line manager, I would present myself, quaking a little. Then, like Balaam’s donkey, I would be momentarily gifted the ability to assert myself. How dare she want to change me, how dare she not accept me for who I essentially was: a sensitive man who, (a), (b), (c) and (d) notwithstanding, more than made up for that with (a), (b), (c) and (d). I was tired of it.


Well in that case, she’d say, I fancy you again, so we’re not breaking up.


Oh, really? That’s great! I’d throw myself at her feet. Shall we go out for a drink? Where would you like to go?


In that case, she’d say, I find your devotion to me nauseating and we’re breaking up again.


The only woman who ever really accepted me for who I was was Angelique. And she wanted to
 
kill
 
me.


I move the five of hearts onto the six of spades.


I look up to see Justine reaching into her coat. She removes her ringing phone from an inside pocket. Frowns as if deciding whether to ignore it or not. Answers it. Listens.


Yes I am, she says.


She laughs.


I can’t stand talking on a train, she says. I’ll call you back.


She sits with her hand still in her book and looks out the window, a little calmer, a very faint smile.


Item.
 
Professor Justine Pearce is objectively desirable and impressive.
 
Item
. I need feel neither proud of her having chosen me nor the frisson of insecurity that she might leave me; there is nothing to have chosen and nothing to leave.
 
Item
. What I get from Justine, and what I never got from any of the sad, stubby psychodramas to which I subjected too many women of my generation, is security and validation.
 
Item.
 
I need never question the purpose of our relationship because I have delegated that to her. So that even if she is toying with me for reasons I may not yet understand, I can trust that it will ultimately improve my performance, or some future performance; it is simply not my problem.
 
Item.
 
Thus, in Justine, I find an escape from the whole question of the self and how she could possibly love me because that question is already answered:
 
She couldn’t.
 
Item.
 
Therefore now that we’re already in the realm of the impossible maybe we can all just relax for a change. Perversely, and to borrow a contemporary platitude, Justine is the first person to really make me feel
 
seen
.


I move the ace of clubs to the foundation.


I start thinking about the word “chihuahua,” a word that abstracts very quickly. Chihuahua chihuahua chihuahua. From one of Dr. Blend’s lectures I know that this is called semantic satiation. To combat this I start thinking about chihuahuas themselves, perhaps because they are very undignified, and that is how I feel. I imagine a film called
 
Last Train to Chihuahuaville
, which would either prominently feature many chihuahuas or not feature a single chihuahua at all, but still have a little chihuahua on the poster.


The train draws into the station and Justine stands, zips
 
The Tetrapharmakos
 
back into the suitcase, puts the pencil behind her ear, fastens a single button on her coat and carries the case to the vestibule.


Plenty of things are not your problem: constructing or demolishing a building, convening a music festival, working in HR. These are not things you should ever worry about.


I must change trains after another three stops. Justine must be traveling for the rest of the day.


Seeing her like this—although I cannot currently see her waiting in the vestibule at the train doors—does not so much makes me miss Justine as it makes me miss black bag, being black bag, being someone she would recognize and be pleased to see.


I wish that I had stuffed black bag into my rucksack so that I could have gone and changed in the train toilet and then nonchalantly walked back into the carriage and waited for her to notice me.


The doors hiss open. She turns when she reaches the platform and this time, with nothing to lose, I stare at her directly, longingly, I make eye contact and she notices and stares back at me. Her lip curls slightly, in a manner that I suppose anyone might affect when they realize a creepy guy is staring at them.


I mouth
 
Justine
, although I have no idea what that looks like to her, aside from more weird and unwanted attention, and I feel momentarily disgusted with myself as the train pulls away and I whip around in my seat and then stand up so that I don’t lose a second, not a possible split second of her presence.


I press myself to the glass.


Maybe I am adding to her worries, the fake student, Michaels, the sense that she is under surveillance.


I can’t help it.


I’m in here, I say. Out loud this time. I’m in here. It’s me.

MY, HOW HIS LEGS HAVE GROWN.
 
Not so cute anymore, are you?


I rub Elliott’s head and he bites my hand.


Little fucker. I’ll take him for a long one.


I finish my second cup of tea, brush a little moss off my parents.




It is not far before the road
 
turns into a path, which turns into a track of mulch, a corridor of trees leaning to console each other, mottled sunlight through the offcuts, then a stile and then fields and fields and fields, grazing land out of season, a dozen stitched-together landscape paintings, as if designed solely for a single ambler’s daydreams, my childhood kingdom.


I mean literally
 
nobody
 
seems to live here except for my parents.


Makes me reflective, puts me in a reflective …
 
mien
.


For fifteen years I have dedicated myself to becoming a character actor. It is a little humiliating to admit that I sought success. That it mattered to me. That I am wounded by its absence.


On some level I must see it as my
 
right
, must be that genre of man who downplays his achievements, not so much out of humility or even false modesty as because they really
 
did
 
come easily to him. Oh pshaw.


Only sans the achievements
, says Elliott.
 
Thus bitter
.


At some point you must make peace with your failure.


No
, says Elliott.
 
You must rage and gnash and cry big, snotty tears. If you feel you haven’t been given your due you must never shut up about it
. He begins to chase his tail but gets bored after two snaps and trots along ahead of me.


That’s a bad look for anyone, Elliott. Grace, above all else.


Nobody is watching you to see if you’ll be graceful or not
, avers Elliott.
 
And what paltry work to concern yourself, first and foremost, with how you come across. What a poor, thin gruel!


Elliott, we are constantly on trial. Even a child is known by his doings.


And a dog by his leavings
.


And a dog by his leavings, yes.


What meager consolation
.


This is where the river cuts through the fields, like an afterthought, the river, scrawled in hurriedly. I swat at a crane fly and turn to look for Elliott.


Don’t bother the duck.


Elliott has frozen in his tracks, mesmerized, as if he cannot believe his luck.


One day
, he growls in falsetto,
 
snap their necks and drink their blood
.


So yes at some point the work becomes the work of
 
failure
, short selling your hope against your disappointment, planning to buy it back later for less money.


What would Justine say?


If you knew that you wouldn’t be missing her
.


I’m not angry about the lack of funding for real art. I’m not even angry with the compromised and well-intentioned who wouldn’t know real art if they saw it. I’m angry with
 
you
 
(yes, you; look up from the page for a moment and frown about it) for not wanting it anymore.


Maybe I am bitter, I say. But I’m surrounded by Philistines.


The Philistines are much maligned
, says Elliott.
 
Good citizens who loved their dogs
.


And what am I left with? Civic theater. Creative partnerships. A multidisciplinary collaboration. A publicly funded project that incorporates several institutions, whimsically overreaches and, in so doing, inadvertently sets astronomy
 
and
 
literature
 
and
 
dance back by several decades. Audience-evaluation questionnaires, patronizing supportive bromides, Biro-ed platitudes from relatives of stakeholders. Everyone gets paid. A bad-tempered man who is wrong about everything—but
 
right about this—might compose a takedown on his blog. Nobody else would bother.


Oh I just can’t, you know?


Better, I think, to audition for Extremely Ordinary Looking and Clumsy Male Strippers of Various Ages.


And if that doesn’t exist, establish it.


That could really take off
, Elliott concedes.
 
People are desperate for a little authenticity.


Desperate for a little flapping penis under an untoned paunch.


For anything real
, says Elliott,
 
however flappy
.




It is possible to be too boring
 
and too interesting at the same time. A French press into which you’ve poured two and a half inches of ground coffee but then only allowed it to brew for thirty seconds.


Glug glug glug
, says Elliott, and disappears through a hedge.


Do I contradict myself?


I decide to let him roam and return to me.


I sit down under a tree and try to enjoy the cold wind nullifying the sunlight, which you can, if you catch the feeling in time.


How beautiful it all is.


Who are you to
 
accept
? It sounds awfully close to toleration.


It is not so much about who gets a seat at the table.


It’s that there shouldn’t be a table. Should never have been a table.


Destroy all tables.

I SLEEP BADLY FOR TWO NIGHTS.


Justine refers to her husband so casually and takes it as read that I needn’t know the first thing about him in order to affect a similar level of indifference. I will put any questions or thoughts of Justine’s husband in a corner of my mind and erect a force field around it made of thoughts of Justine and how wonderful she is and how urgently I want to talk to her or put my hand on her waist. Naturally this solar-powered force field only holds up during daylight hours—it has no battery or means of power storage—and at night I think about little else.


Each night Justine’s husband appears at the side of my bed, looking sorrowful, holding an empty white side plate with some crumbs on it, in hallucinatory clarity.


Sometimes I would see Justine’s husband as a former Olympic rower, his fair hair cropped short, handsome but unappealing face, gherkin-shaped penis and ample balls sculpturally discernible beneath his black and yellow Lycra shorts. A glass of barley water of a man, but still physically imposing, chiseled arms, still rowed, still talked predominantly about rowing,
 
was
 
still rowing, in any given situation was literally rowing, had earned that right. This husband was almost entirely without personality but for a steely irritation that would rise like a valve without warning. A human pair of chinos with a pin somewhere in them. It would be pride or disappointment or he’d catch himself being kind or pleasant company and suspect it wasn’t very masculine so he’d lash out verbally at another man for no reason and spoil their beer because they’d either have to laugh it off or confront him, the latter unwise. Such men are obsessed with status in a way that is quite pitiful, a prestige coffin, but they have a way of corralling you into approving of their shallow dreams
 
and disparaging your own in front of them. And they ask this of you with such childlike insistence, such pathetic trust in life as a competition they can dedicate themselves to and win through discipline, through grit, through a spirited attitude, that it’s tempting just to give it to them—
yes, small parts, really, a minor character actor, but it makes me happy, why yes thank you, if you’re having another—
but it does cost you something. You dwell on it for months afterward and wonder if you could have just told them to go fuck themselves. For the most part I say nothing to them and they consider me beneath contempt. Justine wouldn’t have anything in common with such a man, but might admire the machine of his body and mind, his brain a new and more efficient battery. Justine making a joke that goes over his head; Justine enjoying efficient and pneumatic sex but frustrated by the lack of emotional connection.


Have you finished your toast already, darling? I’d say to this phantom. Do you want another piece?


Sometimes I would see Justine’s husband as a poor old white bear of a professor with sclerosis and a smoking habit she’d roll her eyes at but never draw attention to. A Dryden scholar with a favorite crystal whiskey tumbler, mild mannered and a little bewildered but get him started on the state of academia in the twenty-first century and he’d start rattling like a pressure cooker. He’d drive her for pub lunches in a racing green MG and sometimes at night he’d just stroke her cheek and cry softly. When he fell asleep she’d creep out of the room to write. Stepping over a big white dog who slept twenty-three hours a day. I preferred this husband, but there was an uncomfortable, waxy odor of paternalism to the whole thing; he didn’t really appreciate Justine as a human being: She was some unexpected windfall, some object of worship installed in his ramshackle but beautiful converted farmhouse on the outskirts of the city; someone who could be placed
 
just so
 
on a deck chair in the grounds in a tasteful, flattering summer dress with an indulgently strong negroni he’d mixed her, and observed from an upstairs window, his every breath charged. Justine tentatively placing a foot in the stream. Justine taking a sip of her drink and tilting her head to the sun. Justine adjusting a purple
 
strap under her dress. All of this left me feeling a little nauseated, as if his relationship with her could only be voyeuristic, or was founded upon or reached its full consummation in distance and longing, which I couldn’t imagine being especially fulfilling for her.


Go and put your plate in the sink, will you, there’s a dear, I’d tell him.


Sometimes I would see Justine’s husband as a gifted oncologist, a handsome, charming creep she’d grown tired of and hardly saw anyway because he worked such long hours. Silver fox sleeping through luxury city breaks with Justine, asking precisely quarterly how her work was going, repeatedly sexting a junior colleague then claiming he was a sex addict before the industrial tribunal and going into an extremely well-funded study run by a recovering sex addict because research into sex addiction is never out of fashion. This husband was her age, but played golf. Golf! A museum of himself, so virtuous in his occupation it gave him carte blanche to behave without any consideration for others outside of the clinical setting; in fact his behavior would verge on the abusive. A philanderer who was nonetheless insanely jealous of the attention Justine received from their male friends; who would accuse her of “humiliating him” after dinner parties once they were alone and the mask could slip. Justine fully aware that she was minimizing some very problematic behavior; Justine quietly divesting from their joint bank account.


Don’t hold it like that or you’ll get crumbs everywhere, I’d say to him.


I’d wonder what Justine could possibly see in him that I didn’t, before remembering that they were separated—or something—so she obviously didn’t see much in him any longer, and also that I’d made him up.


Sometimes I would see Justine’s husband as a goaty little fellow with a curly beard. This husband was very funny and a little cruel, and tolerated their arrangement, an arrangement that, for the time being, included
 
me
, with resignation and contempt. This husband was a concert violinist and if anyone talked about anything other than music, or was studying anything other than music, or expressed an interest in anything other than music, he’d say, “Oh
 
dear
” theatrically and look at them with sympathy, with concern. I liked this husband most of all and
 
felt bad for him and wanted, if anything, to get to know him and explain things, if that were even possible, if things could be said to have a satisfactory explanation. Except before I could speak this husband would raise his crumby side plate toward the ceiling and seem about ready to stave my head in with it.

CHECK THIS OUT.


Claudio throws something at me and it bounces off my shoulder.


Sorry.


I kneel to the floorboards to retrieve an enamel pin badge, one inch square. It is dark green and gold and bears an abstract squiggly line that forms the vague shape of a country on a map.


What’s this?


Your movements over three days.


What?


It’s a map, says Claudio, in the form of a fashionable lapel brooch, of your movements over three days.


Cool, I say.


It
 
is
 
cool.


He comes to my side and points at part of the shape.


That’s my place … That’s probably the campus—you can see the line’s a bit thicker where you walk over the same paths over and over again. I’m using you to generate the codes—you know there’s no such thing as a random number? They have to use all kinds of crazy stuff like lightning strikes and bird migration patterns. You—or black bag—is our private random number generator.


And what’s the badge for?


Ah, says Claudio, it’s just a little free gift for early investors.


Are people really investing?


People are so into it! They’re all like, What’s his deal?


Is that really enough?


I’ve had to do a bit of work on the story.


Like what?


You said you didn’t want to know anything, says Claudio. And I don’t want to say anything that might make you self-conscious and affect your performance.


I think about this for a moment.


What are you telling them about me?


I sow little suggestions, says Claudio. Are you a mathematician who went insane trying to solve a theorem?


Hmm.


A disgraced former television actor who fell foul of the MeToo movement?


“Fell foul of”!


So you’re doing penance. Did a conceptual artist buy you for £400,000 and now you have to go around like this for the rest of your life? None of it matters! says Claudio. It’s just speculation.


Jesus, I say.


I need to study probabilities in
 
Dados Mania
 
for several hours tonight, says Claudio. But why don’t you grab an instant margarita and we can spitball a bit more.


Spitball what? More disgrace?


Spitball where the project’s going, says Claudio. We need to think about upscaling, and we need to make sure it doesn’t just fizzle out.


Should I lie down like an analysand again?


Yeah, says Claudio, lie down like a salamander; that seemed to work last time.


I take a pocket-size yellow can from the table and I lie on Claudio’s couch.


Let’s talk about you, he says. What is black bag about for you?


I walk around and nobody can see who I really am.


And how does that feel?


Liberating … Lonely, I say. I mean everybody notices you, but it’s not really
 
you
 
they’re noticing.


And how does that feel to you, personally?


I have to let go of my desire for recognition.


Your desire for recognition as an artist?


My desire, like that of all artists, is that I should be sui generis.


So generous?


Entirely and absolutely myself. Self-created, without parents or place of birth. I want prestige, attention and love and I want to be comforted and looked after, like a god baby.


The God Baby.


But what terrifies me, Claudio, is that, in my heart of hearts, I am nothing more than an English suburbanite. Unus multorum.


Can you stop with the fucking
 
X-Men
 
shit?


In fact, what could be
 
more
 
English suburbanite than wanting to be a successful artist?


I don’t know, says Claudio. I think you sound a bit down on yourself.


And I hate that, I say. I hate belonging to a type. I hate being archetypal.


Nothing archetypal about you, baby, says Claudio. You’re working at the cutting edge of site-specific theater. And the site is the world.


What I fear, I say, is mediocrity and delusion. I fear that I’ve been kidding myself as long as I could articulate it. And this is the first time I’ve ever felt that a role truly uses all of my specific gifts, such as they are. And maybe that’s
 
because
 
the performance is dependent on nobody even knowing it’s me.


If you really know you’re good, you don’t need the praise or the flattery, says Claudio. That’s what I’m hearing.


Nobody knows if they’re good, I say.


But you are, says Claudio. You remember
 
A View from the Bridge
. You remember
 
Arcadia
. You were brilliant. People went crazy!


At
 
school
? I say. Claudio, that’s like putting my pasta collage on the fridge, come on. We had a very lovely and somewhat overambitious drama teacher.


I’m just saying you’re good.


I’m touched you remember.


Okay, says Claudio, closing his notebook. I’m not sure how to use any of that, but it’s good stuff. Maybe one of the potential stories is that you’re
 
you
.

THERE ARE DAYS,
 
you know them too, when you have to remind yourself to be grateful. Maybe you have a little headache, cannot concentrate on a sentence, cannot choose an action to perform with any elegance. On these days I remember a child named Brian my mother cared for once a week. Brian had severe cerebral palsy and if it was a Saturday I’d go along for the planned activity. We’d go to the swimming pool and I’d swim with Brian—he’d lie on his back and I’d hold him underneath the shoulders and pull him up and down the pool turning when the water was too deep for my feet to reach the bottom. He’d laugh and I’d feel profoundly useful. Or we’d go to the coast, where Brian hated the seagulls and I’d run around the chair like a character in a computer game, hollering at the seagulls and waving my arms if they got too close.


Defending Brian from the seagulls was a pure form of purpose, and one that I miss.


On such days you need the little pink skull embroidered on your undershirt. You are already dead. You already know how this ends. On such days it would be very good to be given a large brass or silver bowl to polish. A clattering detachable drawer of cutlery the size of a coffin. Fish knives, soup spoons. A scouring pad, a thick and odorous salve. It would be good to be put to work. Or take to your bed.


I try to make porridge but I do it wrong and it reduces into a knot that I chew on with a black instant coffee. I try to read the paper but get lost between the byline and the verbiage; what do these people want from me?


I sit in my leather chair and stare at the wall. I doze off.


When I wake up I go to the shops and buy a bag of King Edward potatoes, a bottle of Italian red wine and some salted butter.


I go back to my flat and cut an
 
X
 
in the biggest potato and put it directly onto the oven shelf, which I set to 150 degrees. I return to my
 
chair. It is now half past four in the afternoon. The potato will be perfect by half past six.


My phone rings against my breast and I take a video call from Howard at Half a Mind to Kill.


I’m working, Howard.


Mate, he says. I’m sorry it’s been a while since the clusterfuck at Brown’s.


No, no, that’s fine, I say. I’ve been busy with a little long-term thing.


You still want me to call? This is a good one, is all. Detective, conman, art-world thing.


Right.


Two weeks’ time. One night only. A grand.


Now, part of me hoped I had left all of this behind. Pouring my energy into overwrought farces with a murder bolted on and a mystery less satisfying than a cracker puzzle. Catering to an audience I despise and who, if they have any sense at all of the tragedy of human life, probably pity me. The better my performance, the greater their pity. The last few weeks have proved I am meant for better things.


All of that notwithstanding, I have very little to do in the evenings at the moment and I have spent most of Blend’s handouts, generous as they are, paying off some outstanding bills and topping up my cupboards.


I hesitate. I’m in, I say.


That’s great, says Howard. Twenty-eighth. Rehearsal at five for a seven-thirty sitting.


Yeah yeah. I can’t make the rehearsal.


Can you try?


My life depends on this and that’s fucking hilarious.


I’ll try. How are things with you, Howard?


Me
?


Howard, a good functionary, hates to be asked about himself.


My phone starts murmuring
 
boop-boop boop-boop
, and I can see that Justine is calling. I sit up very straight.


I have to take this.


You’re a star, mate, says Howard.




Justine?


Oh, hi!


Are you okay?


I don’t usually call, she says.


You don’t.


But I’m at a
 
wedding
, of all places, she says.


With your husband?


Yes, with my husband.


You’re at a wedding in the Lake District with your husband.


It’s quite fancy, if also the most normcore thing you ever saw, hotel and spa, right on a lake, naturally, little ornamental jetty for sunset photos.


Her voice is muffled and I can hear the
 
chk
 
of a cigarette lighter and Justine exhaling.


I love to smoke at weddings. Yes, so lanterns as the dusk falls, a wooden bridge. I’m champagne drunk. I just want to go for a swim.


Could you?


I’d ruin my hair and my makeup and possibly drown.


These are friends of yours? The wedding?


His, really, she says. But yes, I guess. People I’d maybe expect a little more originality from, in all honesty, but they have some interesting associates. Something funny happened.


I am controlling my breathing, but it is sharp and I feel charged, charged to hear Justine’s voice, which is both glassy and warm, charged that she chose to call me.


What happened?


Something funny happened and I can’t tell anyone so I thought I’d tell you.


I mean I need to be good for something, I say.


Oh you are, she says.


Someone upset you?


No. Well, yes. You know when you realize someone’s checking you out?


You have no idea what I look like, I say.


You’ve been checked out.


On occasion, yes.


Nobody likes being stared at, but it’s okay when it’s a little furtive, she says. It’s nice to be admired. This was … while we were waiting, after the ceremony, when we were queuing for a taxi to take us to the reception.


You and your husband?


Me and my husband and two of his colleagues. This man kept looking back at me. I mean actually this wasn’t furtive at all—he was trying to catch my eye. This man, she says. I don’t know if you ever followed international rugby?


Not especially. I’ll watch it if it’s on.


Do you remember that French player Chabal? From the early 2000s.


Oh, I say. Actually, yes.


For some reason Chabal stands out in my memories of the early twenty-first century just as he did on the field, a marble statue, a third again above the human scale, made flesh. Somebody who was doing exactly what he’d been born to do—it shone in his every violent, balletic movement, a formal poem, a trobar clus, an act of worship in space and time. He made you feel ashamed of yourself, not for anything so crass as your comparative lack of stature or vitality, but for not having found your place in the world as absolutely, for not having committed, with your full throat, your heart, your spirit, so completely to who and what you were.


I’m generally a little indifferent to sport, I say, but yes, I remember him.


Sébastien Chabal, says Justine. Ferocious. They called him the caveman, which I thought was rather unfair because he was actually very beautiful. Someone has long hair and a huge beard and people react to that as if it’s the only thing about him. When actually a man like that wouldn’t look out of place invading Attica or … metamorphosing into a swan.


He was, I concede, very striking.


The thing is, my first thought, when I see a man like that, is how gentle he’d be. He’d be so worried about just breaking someone in two that he’d have got used to being very gentle.


Yes.


So this guy looks like Chabal, which is one thing, but he also has eyes … like these golden yellow amber-colored eyes, like an animal—like he’s …


It’s a very rare eye color.


It is—it’s almost horrifying. He’s in a dark gray suit with a red flower pinned to the lapel. I’m wearing a long black dress with a big slit in both sides.


Mm.


And after the reception he was collecting the glasses and the programs and when he took my glass, he looked at me again and gave my hand a squeeze and I said, I’m Justine, and he said, Yes, and I said, Do you know Therese?—that’s who’s getting married—and he said he was Therese’s half-brother, and I said, Wow, for some reason.


I giggle.


Right? she says. I’m glad I can share this with you.


I get comfortable in my leather chair.


So then we get to the reception and my husband takes my hand to help me out of the taxi and we take glasses of Pimm’s from big silver trays in the lobby and my husband asks if I want a proper drink and I say, Yes, of course, so he goes to the bar and I can see him glad-handing a bunch of people, in his element, you know. And I feel sort of happy and sort of resentful at the same time. And almost
 
immediately
 
Jonathan wants to feel me out about a bioethics symposium in Brock, and I down my Pimm’s and eat a little square of cucumber. Like, Jesus, Jonathan, take an afternoon off? Classic wedding stuff. I mean we’re all here but we’re not here, you know? We’re all like, This is nice isn’t it? With a slightly raised eyebrow, So how
 
are
 
you? Oh
 
no
, I’m so
 
sorry
. I’m at that stage in my life, says Justine, where all the news starts getting a little worse, you know?


I don’t, I say.


But you can imagine.


Oh, I can imagine.


That’s something you’re good at, I’ve noticed. But the thing is, I can’t see this man anywhere. Like where the fuck has he gone?


And I’m bored and he basically nonverbally promised me something so I’m a little disappointed—I keep scanning the clusters of guests hoping to see him towering over someone, being nice. There are, what, three or four rooms? One with white tables for the dinner, one with the bar and a big square open space, a terrace with heat lamps, a room with stacked chairs where lots of people have spilled over even though it doesn’t feel like it’s been specially prepared. Everyone drinking, nervously, trying to strike the right balance between cynicism and wit. And he’s just nowhere to be seen, so I keep circulating, pretending that I’m doing the rounds, but I’m not: I’m looking for him. My husband occasionally appears and gives me a gin and tonic.


And maybe almost two hours has passed by this point. And I’m a little drunk now, and I’m laughing with Daniella—sorry, I’m not going to bore you by introducing everyone.


No, that’s fine.


About work stuff, some meaningless amendment they’ve made to assessment criteria, and I’m saying something completely inane like
 
It’s just nice to have a change of scene, isn’t it?
 
when I feel a
 
giant
 
hand on my back.


Uh-oh.

marches

There’s a curved bar in the corner with taps and optics. Big oil painting of a stormy seascape. Dark wooden tables. You know these venues—they have about seventeen different function rooms and he’s found a disused one.

Because of the slits.

Right.

IN THE LECTURE I SIT
 
in my usual aisle seat behind the young woman who smells like shoe polish and Tom from the seminar group. The lecture theater is cooler and brighter than usual, which may be down to a flaw or a power surge in both the air-conditioning and lighting units. Nonetheless, everyone is alert, and an atmosphere of heightened concentration pervades the room.


The raw data I am asking you to read for this week’s seminars comprises one hundred and forty-four brief accounts of “falling in love,” says Blend, and I would like you to identify your top three and be prepared to explain why.


Yes?


One hundred and forty-four, yes.


I know this may feel like a lot of reading, but it is the best preparation for the kind of datasets you will be dealing with professionally.


You will be able to download the PDF from the virtual learning environment.


Today we’re going to be talking about initial attraction and the maintenance of—or
 
current
 
of—attraction. Which, like an electrical current, fluctuates, yes?


Back in Sweden, alongside my job in the sawmill, I have started taking Swedish lessons and volunteering as a reader for people in comas. This delights Birgitta and confirms the good nature she had already projected onto me. Or I have done it in order not to disappoint her. Or I was bored and worried that I was becoming an alcoholic. Sluta oroa dig. She pours me a tin cup of akvavit.


I wonder why Birgitta has taken such an interest in me. Whether I remind her of a relative from the distant past … Her favorite cousin, Lennart, who died of pneumonia at the age of thirteen.


We all remind each other of someone.


A relative from her distant past invisibly escorted back to her. Molded just so in another culture, unaware that each inconsequential experience, every silly thing that happened to me and that I therefore chose, made me more and more like Lennart. Lennart, she felt, was often misunderstood. Not quick, and often quiet, but more perceptive than most, in his way.


And had I not moved to Sweden, I never would have known.


This is why Birgitta looks at me fondly at unpredictable moments. When I struggle to take off my boots or scald myself by doing the washing up in very hot water. When I laugh too frequently or agreeably.


She is thinking, How like Lennart he is.


Given our nature, even the ways in which I am not at all like Lennart will somehow remind her of Lennart or serve to make the ways in which I
 
am
 
like Lennart more compelling. A third helping of kalops. But no fat on me at all.


Oh, Birgitta. During moments like these I have to remind myself that you don’t exist.

CLAUDIO BUZZES ME IN AND
 
meets me in his overlit pine hallway. I remove my shoes. I drop my bus copy of Henry James’s
 
The Europeans
 
on his coffee table. What I love, in James, is the way people fancy each other. He wrote that better than almost anyone. Also wickedness. The way people embrace wickedness because after a certain point why bother trying to be good?


You been away again? I couldn’t get through.


I was doing some black bag stuff—this is an important moment, Claudio says. We’re starting to get some outside interest.


In the clip art drawings you showed me?


Among other stuff, says Claudio. But it’s hard to make time with my schedule.


A million pharaohs with whips and one poor Claudio.


He is currently broadcasting a game called
 
Another Fine Mess
, where you alternate between playing Laurel or Hardy in a series of escalating adventures.


Claudio picks up
 
The Europeans
, flicks through it, sniggers and throws it back on the table.


Male novelists, he says.


James? I say. The Master? He’s hardly representative.


Well, there’s plenty of them, says Claudio. Thing is, homes, men aren’t even
 
reading
 
novels anymore. To be honest I’d respect them less if they did.


Why’s that?


Dude reading a novel. C’mon. I spit on him.


No, Claudio, I do not agree with this at all.


Dude all sitting there in his chinos frowning in concentration, turning pages.


I’m horrified by this side of you.


Scribbling a little annotation in the margin with a specially sharpened pencil. All like, Ooh, how piquant.


Claudio pushes an imaginary pair of spectacles up his nose.


Men read novels, Claudio. I read novels.


You’re different, says Claudio. You’re a scholar.


Scholars are people too. And I’m not a scholar.


Aside from you, a teenage boy might—a depressed teenage boy might read some doomer sci-fi. After the pupal stage, it’s gonna be … Once a year, maybe. Biography of Napoleon,
 
Subtle Art of Not Giving a Fuck
, a book about how great trees are … The men who read novels are the same men who bewail the lost art of dressing for dinner.


I look out Claudio’s windows. A red light blinks on the nose of a crane. It is building a sixteen-story block of flats for the men of the future to not read novels in.


Well, shit, I say. I guess you’re right.


And they’re
 
much
 
more likely to just collect vinyl, he says. If they had to pick one. Or bake sourdough. Vis-à-vis men, it’s a dead hobby. You’d have a bigger audience if you made … bow ties.


This philistinism doesn’t become you.


Harris tweed with a copy of
 
Candide
 
sewn into the left pocket.


Gah.


Protruding just so.


What I want to suggest, I say, is that we ought to resist this downhill slide into total barbarism.


Resist how?


And if there’s nothing material we might do, we must at least resist it via
 
hope
.


Hope for what?


Hope that better things are coming. And that when they come, it would be a shame if we’d given up.

ON THE WAY TO LECTURE THEATER 3
 
I get waylaid by a group of students in the foyer who call me “mate.” I wonder if intimidating people know that they are intimidating; that their affect is one of intimidation; that they became intimidating as children and have been so ever since, from the cradle to a place prepared for them in a sports bar; if they wake up in the morning and crack their knuckles and look forward to a good day of being vaguely menacing to everyone they meet, a day of unilateral disparagement and implied violence. Or if it’s just a whim that seizes them every now and then.


The boys tell me that they’ve noticed me around and they want to know what the deal is. And while this is a perfectly reasonable request and one I should, if I’m honest, expect, I find their tone off-putting and feel disinclined to answer.


We should remember that the saints, in their extreme forbearance, their love for all humanity, their embracing of mockery and scourges, would still avoid a conversation if they sensed that the vibes were off. I do not know if I believe in any of this, the whole metaphysical host, but I know which side I’d choose.


One of them is holding a large chicken-and-sweet-corn baguette, obscenely open to the world.


Is it a protest?


Were I to paint him as an icon he would brandish that baguette beneath his gurning face.


I don’t say anything.


Or some nonbinary thing—new gender just dropped?


I stand with the three boys and say nothing.


Do you even talk? Is that part of it?


Can we just say whatever we want to you?


I try not to shrug. I don’t want to lay claim to anything. If I could be black bag by myself in a room forever I would do that, but what would be the point? It’s inherently relational.


How hollow and foul is the heart of man, I say to them, and turn on my flippered heel.




An ill English light,
 
puce skies and fluorescents. Something is wrong with me today; I’m not feeling it. I doubt my own commitment, doubt my abilities regardless of my commitment. In the lecture room I am so grateful to sit next to the young woman who smells of shoe polish that I would like to say hi and hug her. Instead I bow to her and she nods back.


Dr. Blend enters the room two minutes late.


I am so sorry.


He sounds a little defeated, as if someone has turned down his volume.


He puts his worn brown satchel on a stool and flicks on the projector, then changes his mind and flicks it off again.


Misery, he begins, is the norm. We must accept this. With this in mind, we must understand exactly how sensitive we are. Embarrassingly sensitive, to the last one of us. And petty and vain and vindictive. We like to accuse one another of being oversensitive because all of us are. “A trifle consoles us because a trifle upsets us,” says Pascal.


Think of the moment, following the 2008 subprime crisis, when cupcakes proliferated like a cancer. Think of the way the icing became more elaborate and eventually larger than the cakes themselves, beehive hairdos of icing.


In such a symbol, what are the bees?


We are the bees, I think.


Vi lever som bin. Birgitta nods, pouring water into a pan to boil. One of the walls of her cabin rattles in the wind and I fear for us come midwinter.




At the end of the lecture
 
Dr. Blend removes a round hat box from his bag and opens it to reveal a bright, iced-pink cake.


Made with several eggs, he tells us.


I count nine students out of two hundred who go and take a slice before leaving the lecture theater. Maybe they are moved to take a piece of cake as a sign, an unspoken commitment to Dr. Blend and his unorthodox vocal style. I watch them laugh and briefly, casually chat with him.


Do this in remembrance of me.




I hope that Dr. Blend is okay.
 
Perhaps it is normal, when somebody is talking to you, that after a while you start to think, I wonder if this person is okay. Usually they turn it around and, with some gesture, some formula of words or phrases, some incremental change in their tone of which they are perhaps not even aware, they reassure you that they are, after all, okay, and that you are not witnessing the live disintegration of a human spirit. Perhaps this is also what a cake is for.

The Orb of Homuncules
 
is a very sexual, very violent film in the “blockbuster” genre with a running time of two hours and thirty-four minutes.


We are one of three couples catching the preview matinee. Justine guides me into the third row and I collapse into the soft chair, leather on leather, enfolded.


The Orb of Homuncules
 
is set in an amalgamation of medieval England and a future technocratic dystopia. And it is a world wherein every single time you masturbate to climax a very noticeable, permanent and unsightly blemish appears on your neck, a series of marks that gradually climbs to your face, with the result that most people are covered in innumerable disfiguring sores on their neck and face.


A small elite, the Clean, is apparently able to resist self-pleasure and they naturally find themselves in a position of authority over the general population. This they exercise with a seeming magnanimity whilst making no secret of the fact that they quietly view their citizens as little better than beasts enslaved to their vulgar appetites. It turns out that the Clean, who number forty-seven members, are simply having their pustules and blemishes surgically removed or cosmetically hidden. Also they are involved in human trafficking and kidnapping, and they sexually
 
assault members of the public under the guise of a “lottery” whereby people enter and the winners are selected for purification and to join the lower orders of the Clean, to live with them in the citadel and, if not rule, then at least administrate as part of their equivalent of the civil service. But really, when someone is selected in the “lottery,” the Clean are just imprisoning them in rooms in the citadel, taking turns to sexually assault them, which, oddly, doesn’t cause spots to appear on your neck or face and is seen, even among the Clean themselves, who despite all this cannot seem to forego masturbation altogether and require secret underground hospitals full of surgeons to remove and disguise their blemishes, to be the superior act.


Anyway, that’s just the backdrop and little is done to explain it. The point is, some people are starting to cotton on to what’s really happening and there’s a growing resistance movement, so the bulk of the story is a quest narrative to rescue an enchanted glowing blue orb from the deepest cave in the ten kingdoms. Homuncules is a former monk who still wears a tattered black habit and who is overweight, disastrously uncoordinated and experiences frequent breathing difficulties, an unlikely hero to say the least, but the only one in possession of the arcane knowledge and spiritual “powers” to locate and access the cave and advise on the perils within. Homuncules is joined by a “crew” of five, selected for their physical beauty—albeit covered in weeping sores—dexterity and strength, and portrayed by vaguely familiar approximations of famous actors. None identical, but crossed with one another to produce the aura of recognizable celebrity.


The orb will restore the natural order, heal everyone’s plague of masturbatory boils, and bring an end to the tyranny of the Clean once and for all. Its cold blue light will reveal the Clean for who they really are. And a new age will dawn.


In the final scene they reach the sacred inner chamber, but Homuncules drops the orb and it shatters into a thousand shimmering pieces on the cavern floor.


It was the barbarian who threw it, casually, and before he did he said, Hey, Homuncules? Catch!

Black bag turns, clocks me and kicks their basket around the corner, as if in a great hurry.


I push my trolley toward them.


They kick their basket around the corner of the cereal aisle and follow the basket out of my sight.


Hey, I say again. Hey, just wondered if you need some help?


When I round the corner there is no sign of black bag. I frown and push my trolley to the next aisle—teas, coffees, hot chocolates, herbs and spices. Black bag is not here either.


I keep walking and eventually I double back on myself and revisit the bakery section, and when black bag isn’t there I am forced to admit that I made the wrong call—they could be anywhere in the supermarket by now.


Baby. Detergents. Nothing.


Perhaps I can cut them off at the exit.


I abandon my trolley by the special-offer crisps.


There is nobody by the automatic doors.


Claudio, hey, I text. I saw another black bag. Call me.


I wait by the toy dispensers. Slimy little kittens in an egg. Bejeweled Mario keychains.


At five minutes past four I see black bag traveling down the trolley escalator toward me. I sidle behind the machines and watch him leave by the automatic doors. I let a mother and child go first then pop my head out. To my right, black bag is ambling out of the car park and back toward the main street.


Oi!


Black bag gathers pace and hustles down a side lane.


I give chase. I can move when I need to.


Black bag is surprisingly quick given their bare feet.


I emerge onto the main road and look left, then right. Picking their way between pedestrians, aware of their inability to blend in, black bag is maintaining a steady pace, clearly in a hurry but eager—insofar as that is possible—not to look as though they are running away.


You! The bag! I’m not trying to scare you I just want to talk!


I run, sometimes on the pavement, sometimes in the road when it’s clear. I feel powerful and unrestricted, playing the part of an ordinary thirty-something.


I pursue black bag into a cobbled alleyway. Dim yellow lights. Each shop has a matching painted cream sign with its name and logo. A vinyl record store, a luxury coffee roaster, vintage clothes boutique.


I watch black bag disappear into one of the last shops on the right, which, as I draw level, I can see is called Chance’s Perfumery.


A bell rings as I push the door. Enormous glass jars full of amber liquid. Too many scents to pick out, like trying to listen to someone else’s dream.


I am out of breath and probably look quite awful.


Did someone in a black bag come in here?


The woman behind the counter shakes her head.


Okay, I say. Sorry.


A black bag? she says.


It must have been another shop, I tell her. I head back outside.


I am opposite a store that sells small glass ornamental animals. I briefly consider the brain feel of giving one’s life entirely to the design, craft and sale of small glass ornamental animals. Last on the left is a dark and dusty bottle shop, which is closed.


Fuck.


Then with a tinkle of bells, black bag emerges tentatively from the glass-animal shop, freezes when they see me.


You! I say, by now with a little spite.


Black bag hobbles then takes off across the street and down the next alley, less salubrious, the backs of restaurants and cafés, their steaming vents.


I run after them despite my burning lungs.


Will you just
 
stop
?


I am unsure why I feel such antipathy toward the other black bag. Either I see them as an imposter or competition for my performance as black bag, or it is purely that they are running away and I am trying to catch them: This relationship alone is bound to produce frustration.


It’s possible they know something about Claudio.


Black bag hops across the next road, and a white van, careening through the narrow lane, temporarily obscures my view. When it passes I can see that black bag has stopped and is leaning against a red skip. I fast-walk toward the skip, brace myself with one hand on the brick wall and try to get my breath back.


For goodness’ sake, I say to black bag. Why are you running away from me?


Why are you
 
chasing
 
me?


Black bag has a reedy, male voice.


Who are you?


Black bag says nothing.


Why are you doing this?


Black bag says nothing but crumples slightly.

Why does everyone hate me? he says, quietly.

He is probably going out to pick up stationery supplies for his recordkeeping.

I AM SHOCKED TO SEE
 
a young man at the front of the lecture theater. He is wearing some expensive- looking wire-framed spectacles, a pressed white shirt and blue chinos. The young man is quite stout, but, I think, healthy. He would be in better shape, but he has to work so hard and it keeps him at his desk. But for that he would have a rower’s physique. He puts his messenger bag on the lectern and takes out a pile of photocopied handouts. He is visibly shaking, but keeping it under control.


The students in the audience exchange concerned whispers and glances.


Thomas, in the next row but one, looks over his shoulder at me and touches his eyebrow twice. I do not know what this means.


Everyone, hey, I’m Dr. Mazower, the young lecturer says. Oliver. Ollie. I realize it can be quite disconcerting when a tutor changes even halfway through a course let alone a week from the end. But we’ll cope, won’t we?


He smiles nervously.


Today I thought we’d just talk over the exam questions and we can go through some model answers. Does that sound okay?


Nobody says anything, but some people nod.


Dr. Mazower projects five sample exam questions on the board.


There are some murmurs of concern.


Just try to narrow it down, says Dr. Mazower. If any of them leave you cold, ignore them. Find one that you have some interest in and we’re probably talking a couple of long days in the library revising, no more than that. I’ll talk you through doing a literature review, compiling your own crib sheet. Okay? You’ve looked at Frenning, presumably?


Silence.


Not looked at Frenning, he says, puzzled. Are you quite sure?


Some of the students obligingly turn back through their notes. One of them looks up and shakes his head.


Well, stop me if this is going over old ground, says Dr. Mazower. Attraction theory was established by Samuel Frenning. The psychologist. Does that ring any bells?


Nobody says anything so he carries on elucidating the basics of attraction theory.


It is as if Dr. Blend had split open like a chrysalis and a younger, more coherent, more student-focused lecturer stepped out. Everyone around me takes notes in a frenzy as if making up for lost time and it does, to be fair, seem as though Blend rather neglected his own syllabus and basic learning outcomes.


Given that I am not submitting an assignment for this module—and that I no longer know quite what my purpose is in the room or on the campus without Dr. Blend to observe me or the results of my presence—I strive to blot out Dr. Mazower’s words.


My thoughts turn to Claudio.


Once I have thought something through in detail it is impossible for it to happen, so I try not to imagine anything good.


This is difficult. You have to start by imagining something good.


A quiet Californian skate park surrounded by palm trees in the evening light, a dark blue that lightens to merge with the orange clouds on the horizon. Five birds fly west.


And then ruin it.


Claudio falls horribly from his skateboard and breaks his wrist.


And then move on to worse.


Claudio: terrified on salvia divinorum in a distant squat, a woman with no teeth approaches saying, It’s alllllll right, numpty. Claudio: handcuffed to a basement radiator. Claudio: buried in an unmarked grave.


I dedicate five minutes’ meditation to each scenario to make sure they can’t happen.


Please be okay.


Please.


God, please.


Something is jamming my signal, and I think it might be the insistent clarity of Dr. Mazower’s lecturing style.


He is talking about the “weak constraint.”


I try to go back to Birgitta in the notional Swedish town.


There is an otherworldly stillness to the cabin—it looks like a photograph.


I call her name, but I can tell nobody is there—it’s as if my voice disturbs the dust. The kitchen has been cleaned meticulously. I rap my knuckles on the silver skillet.


I tie tennis rackets to the bottom of my boots and hike down Birgitta’s snowy hill, then I walk through the streets, my empty sleeve lashed to my shoulder. People look away when they see me.


Say what you like about the death of community values; at least there’s nothing to be
 
cast out of
 
anymore.


I am approaching the bar where I might check in with a couple of colleagues when a man with a droopy gray mustache puts his hand on my shoulder.


Lennart? he says.


I nod. Why not?


He says something in Swedish. He looks perplexed when I don’t understand.


Ah, he says. Birgitta, he says, and looks at me solemnly. He slowly closes his eyes.


I understand that between now and the last time I thought about her, Birgitta has died.


Months have passed, in fact.


I look at the paving stones and he gives my shoulder a squeeze, says something conciliatory in Swedish and carries on down the road.


I wipe a tear.


Själv och ensam.


I know what you’re thinking: You made her up! Just imagine her alive again!


But you know that’s not how it works, you know this is as real as you or me, the great irreversible.


I stand at Birgitta’s grave, holding a bundle of forget-me-nots.


What did she always say? Death is God’s broom. Death is the last door.


I wonder if I should contact the police. About Claudio, I mean.


Mazower wraps up his talk asking students to contact him if they have any questions. I feel a presence over me as the students leave. I look up to see Sophie. Not other Sophie. The original.


I got a First! she says. In my assignment! I know the exam’s sixty percent of the marks, but still. I thought you should know, because you helped me so much with it. I didn’t know what I was doing.


I look at Sophie. She seems very happy. She smells of vanilla essence.


I know you can’t speak, she says.


I stamp my foot once.


Some of us have been talking about getting a drink after the final session, she says. I don’t know if you’re into that sort of thing.


I stamp my foot again.


Well, we’ll let you know, she says. Thanks again. I just wanted to … She takes off her shoulder bag and puts it on her other shoulder. Thanks, she says.

Half an hour’s walk from

THIS IS NOT THE USUAL

Generally speaking, I am not good with computers, but Claudio once taught me how to do this when we couldn’t find our friend’s car after a night out. The latter part of the route appears to trace the canal for several miles, with no roads or tracks, as if the derelict complex has been cut off from civilization and might only be accessible by narrow boat. I pace around my flat,

At first, lacking anyone with whom I can compare notes, I do not feel affected in the slightest. I think I will get myself a glass of water, but then find I cannot get out of the chair. What feels like great physical effort produces, in my limbs, no movement whatsoever.


Claudio, for fuck’s sake, I say.


The feeling is one I have had before, dissociative, the place part of you floats off to when you faint. The same reluctance to return. A little
 
synesthesia. A honk from a chair leg in the apartment above produces a rhombus. A passing car, a conical snakeskin. We ought not be too disappointed if a hallucination resembles cheap head shop artwork when the cheap head shop artwork is, after all, a clumsy attempt to accurately illustrate a hallucination. Perhaps it is more like visiting America for the first time as an English person and feeling like you are in a film. That kind of familiarity. False home. Real home. When I close my eyes I am rushing through something vast and templelike, the inside of a piano the size of a city. The mechanical sounds are probably just a distortion of my own breathing or my own blood. But when I open my eyes I am still rushing through its endless chambers, toothlike and perfect, metallic ziggurats crumbling into natural formations. It’s fine as long as I don’t start thinking I can control it.


Snake knots. Snake knots constricting every time I think I can control it. They feel like an urgent warning.


I am vigilant for any message from the entities or for the room to transform into a library with the great book of Claudio somewhere on its shelves.


The arches and windows and protuberances feel like part of a built environment, metal and enamel, finely sanded and scrubbed to an impossible sheen, although to build anywhere so immense would take thousands of civilizations and a planet too big to exist without collapsing in on itself. The holes are patterned and regular, like fine lace, and then sickeningly erratic like tripe or lung tissue. But then, within them, clean white hall after clean white hall. Sometimes I rush into what appeared to be a scab of mold on an immaculate white surface, but that turns out to be the entrance to the next network of cathedrals.


I feel as though this has been going on long before I was born and long after my death.


Now you see.


Now I see what?


The way the entities speak to me is neither audible nor written, but comes to me like a memory, in the form of the structures they transform into.


You see the great sadness of man and how to express it.


No, I don’t think I do.


Go and find your friend.


Is he okay? I say. Is Claudio okay?


I sweep right into a jagged, barren plane teeming with what at first appear to be horses but then seem more like giant insects clambering over each other at high speed, stepping on one another, using one another as landscape, consuming each other.


Before I can utter a word I have disappeared within one of the folds of a single insect’s exoskeleton and am back flying through crenelated natural steeples and layers of metallic, gauzy tripe.


Maybe once I would have been disturbed by such an incident, but not now.


I am a lid. Or a cork. Or a thumb stuffed into a fissure in the world, in the air of the world. I am the only thing keeping the … I must keep very still. You will not come to a final room where you have to face yourself; this is already yourself. It is already talking to you and this is the only language it knows, a language you used to know.
