I WAKE UP TO WHAT
 
my phone tells me is Saturday lunchtime. I feel fine, really, as if I’ve left a play during the interval because it was no good, but then realized that I was
 
in
 
the play and shouldn’t have left, but then remembered that my character was
 
supposed
 
to leave during the interval and that this was in the script. I’m supposed to just go and live my life.


I step into my flip-flops and repaste the coordinates into my map. It predicts a three-hour walk and a two-hour swim. The place is seemingly inaccessible by public transport or bicycle.




The city side of the canal
 
was redeveloped some years ago into a joyless arcade of chain restaurants and oversized bars. It is a nice afternoon and people are getting shit-faced on outside tables. I pass an expensive burger restaurant where the burgers are too thick to eat. Bottomless brunch. Downsized platters and infinite drinks for thirty quid. The last bone late capitalism intends to throw us.


Some men have arranged two tables outside the Pitcher & Piano so that the second table juts into the towpath and I have to cleave very close to it as I pass, almost upsetting a pint, which laps over its edges and spills a little onto the chair of a muscular brute.


Ohhhh.


One spends so long on the peripheries of traditional masculinity it’s easy to forget people like this still exist.


All right?


It is never easy to capture the tone of an aggressor: the background TV whine it sets off in your brain, synapses firing. I see myself reflected in the canal, a square of white sky and a black parallelogram.


Do you know what, though? he says.


Do you know what, some people don’t have any say in people like me starting on them. They can’t help it. But you’re
 
choosing
 
this.


Two of the men sing, If you’re gonna get battered, clap your hands.


If you’re gonna get battered, clap your hands.


If you’re gonna get battered, if you’re gonna get battered,


If you’re gonna get battered, clap your hands.


He squares up to me.


I mean what are you playing at?


Going around like that?


Despite my heart beating and wishing I was elsewhere or walking on, I feel a curious detachment. As if paused, as if this is already a memory and I can walk around in it, taking measurements; I can already decide how I will narrate it later, and differently for different audiences; I could be self-piteous or impressively unruffled by the experience, depending. Judgmental or magnanimous toward my persecutor, who knows not what he does.


It is only when I think of Justine, and then of my mother, or of anyone who might wish me a better afternoon, that I feel upset.


I can see that he isn’t entirely sure where to hit me. Instead he puts an arm around the middle of black bag, lifts me off the ground and tosses me into the canal.


The sound of the other men laughing subsides as I sink below the surface.


I try to move my arms but the wet leather of black bag keeps them pinned to my chest. I kick my legs but succeed in only turning over under the water so that I can’t detect which way is up.


It occurs to me that I could drown.


I urinate involuntarily, billows of warmth that rush up black bag and reach my face. A sweet taste.


If I could unzip or crawl out of black bag, emerge naked then jump back in to retrieve black bag … My arms are pinned to my sides.


Maybe this is how it ends.


I thrash to the surface and take as much of a breath as I can with black bag tight to my face. I try to move my mouth parallel with one of my eyeholes but succeed only in sucking in the leather.


Goodnight, sleep tight, et cetera.


I can hear raised voices.


I go under again.


I remember at school sometimes people would say, No, it’s tight, if someone was tormenting you.


“Tight” meant “cruel” in nineties slang. Perhaps it was local. Possibly derived from “tight” meaning tight-fisted, miserly, misinterpreted to refer to unkind or unnecessary behavior. Funny thing, language.


But “tight” was used in very specific circumstances, when the victim was considered beneath contempt, too easy a target, clearly incapable of standing up for themselves and already humiliated by their very being.


Don’t do that to him—it’s tight.


I remember it would be said with a certain affection for the antagonist—we hate victims. We
 
could
 
hurt them more and maybe, given all the above, we should!


Anyway—do you know, I think I may actually be drowning? However will I convey this message to you then?—the easiest thing you can take from anyone is their peace of mind. You can do it with one text message. Doesn’t take much to get any of us on the hook; decades of film noir teach us that; all of us femmes fatales and hopeless rubes in our own forgettable way, waiting for the right conditions.


I feel a pinch then a wrench in the center of black bag and something drags my back across a hard surface.


I blink several times and through a corner of my eyehole I can see a woman with shaved blonde hair kneeling over me. She puts her hands on my chest and is about to start doing compressions but I manage to sit up.


I should call the police! she shouts over the canal.


Yeah, whatever, sweetheart.


We should call you an ambulance, she says to me.


She pats my sides looking for some way to remove black bag.


I think … I’m fine, I say, and cough.


I vomit some canal water into black bag.


Thank you, I say, for pulling me out.


Look at you—what are you doing? Are those your friends? Did they put you in that?


No. And no. This is just me.


Can you stand?


She helps me to my feet.


I am, overall, unperturbed. This is not something that happened to me, but to black bag. This is the sort of thing black bag would have to deal with frequently. All of this is instructive.


The sense of restriction, as if some kind of invisible or circumstantial barrier prevents me from reaching it, makes me all the more certain that this is what I must do. That this is the only way I can track down Claudio.


Black bag is goal oriented. To a fault, perhaps. But that is one of their qualities.


No, I do not need to go to a faux Italian restaurant, but I really am grateful.


No, I really am fine.


I would quite like to stop talking.


I’ll walk with you. Where are you going?


This concern for a stranger, this absence of querying my appearance, swells my heart.


No. I’ll be fine. But you’re a saint, I tell her.


I could, of course, write the day off and head home, get washed and dry and go to bed, but black bag will soon dry in the sun during the remaining six-mile walk.




An hour out of town,
 
a single narrow boat called
 
Calliope
 
passes me. There is a man at the tiller with a wide face and a short gray beard. He is wearing a straw hat and sunglasses. He waves at me and I bow.


I feel fine now, really. Can barely taste the canal anymore.


On the waterside the fences bulge with tall purple flowers and dog daisies. The backs of buildings; behind the scenes of a Potemkin village.
 
Then, for over a mile, a postindustrial wasteland of obscure abandoned machines and terrain in sudden deserts.


With two miles to go, the towpath comes to an abrupt end. The canal forks in two and an old white sign points in three directions but, presumably, only for people in boats.


I take an overgrown set of concrete steps to a dirt footpath, but I cannot see a bridge anywhere and, in any case, there is no path on the other side of the canal either. Farther along, a tall, flowerless weed has broken through some old tarmac and sways obscenely. A worn wooden sign reads
 
BOATHOUSE
 
and points to a big corrugated-iron shack to my left.


The boathouse’s reinforced chicken-wire door is bolted with a combination padlock, but the side of the warehouse is warped, leaving a significant gap between the sheet metal side wall and the corner joist. It is just big enough for me to squeeze through with a little discomfort. I hold my breath and try to make myself as slight as possible. Black bag insulates me from the rough edges of the metal. I am, nonetheless, afraid of damaging black bag. I wriggle and get my legs then my shoulders, sideways, through the gap. My head won’t quite fit and after two painful attempts I lower myself to the ground, my neck held in the pincer of the wall and the joist, where the opening is widest. This does the trick. I stand up and hop from foot to foot in relief.


Light breaks through a large crude double door at the back, which opens directly onto the water. I like the image of a little rowboat, Charon-like, but a Charon who has given up on the crossing and propels himself downstream, god knows where. But black bag cannot row. In the boathouse dozens of blue, red and green canoes hang vertically from tall racks. There is a modest inflatable speedboat, deflated, with its motor pointing upward. Crates of plastic oars in neon orange and neon green. Two racks of cracked wetsuits in various sizes. In the corner, supporting a bale of barbed wire, there is an old, dirty swan pedal boat.

81




AS BLACK BAG MY MOVEMENTS ARE,
 
by necessity, a series of nudges. This is how I impose my will on the material world, and it changes the way it looks. I have to use my body like a kind of vertical snake. Insert myself between the roll of barbed wire—grateful for its frayed layers of insulating plastic—and the pedal boat, wriggle under it and then thrust until I am standing again and it topples over. The crash is a musical chord, but atonal or natural, like the drone of a Byzantine chant.


The pedal boat itself is on its side and leaning against the wall of the warehouse. The Formica is stained gray and mildewed, the hollows are festooned with cobwebs, but it appears to be largely intact. There is a crack in the swan, and a piece missing from its neck, but nothing that renders it unseaworthy. Its beak is still bright orange and its face, like any swan’s face, real or plastic, resembles that of a man who doesn’t really want to be at the masquerade ball. I maneuver myself between the swan boat and the wall, which scratches my back. I slide down the wall and the pedal boat rights itself with my body half underneath. I writhe out from under it.


The swan pedal boat is heavy but, using my full force, I am able to push it with my feet and my knees around six inches at a time, pausing to catch my breath after every third push. It is a two-person vessel, but the pedals are only on the driver’s side. These are dirty and rusty. There is a corroded black rubber steering handle, which I will not be able to use.


The great doors of the boathouse open directly onto the water so I need only shove the boat, inch by inch, to the lip of the exit and then, with my remaining strength, force it up to the fulcrum so that it overbalances. The swan pedal boat hits the surface, bobs, takes on a little water
 
then stabilizes. I watch for spirals of bubbles in the ship floor, half expect to see it gurgle and sink to the bottom. It does not.


I step tentatively into the boat. It bucks and rolls and takes on a little more water as I find my feet and then sit, as if this is where I always ought to have been, in the soggy molded-plastic chair. It is hard to pedal in flip-flops so I kick them off. The pedals are rusty and sharp, but the soles of my feet have toughened sufficiently and after a creaking start I am able to get the mechanism moving, and the pedal boat lurches forward quicker than I had expected. I stop pedaling and turn around to see a tiny wake of foam from its manual propeller. We have already drifted a couple of yards from the boathouse.


The sun is low in the sky and enchants the grim, largely functionless buildings, the finger-wagging weeds and pyramidal flowers. Streams of pink cloud. The swan’s great neck, its implacable expression, are picked out in golden light.


I find a rhythm and the swan settles into its natural pace of exactly two-thirds of the average walking speed. This ought to make my total journey less than two hours. I cannot steer the swan so sometimes it connects with the left bank of the canal, but pedaling produces sufficient pressure to turn it a little so that it gradually drifts across and connects with the right bank.


Uncurated plants, the ones we don’t want or barely categorize, grown high as a forest, leaves shaped like symbols from dead religions, dark and dusty. Parts of the shoreline are like this. Other parts are compacted dirt and bits of broken machine from a 1950s sci-fi film, functionless bollards garlanded with murder bouquets. Murder bouquets, fresh and antique, for the poor souls who make this their habitat. Rheumy cellophane and dried brown flowers. A man who swipes his fiancée some murder bouquets.


I am, of course, gambling on the assumption that the warehouse, my destination, will reveal anything at all to me about Claudio’s whereabouts.


This is not something I can think about too much while maintaining the effort required to keep pedaling.


Nonetheless, the scene is one of such tranquility I might console myself that people could
 
choose
 
to do this for recreation or exercise, physical and mental. I stop pedaling for a moment and the swan boat drifts with its own momentum. We all have energies and we have to decide where to direct them. We make these decisions constantly and build whatever acceptable fictions around ourselves we can. We look to each other for reassurance that our decisions have not been, after all, completely arbitrary or predetermined. Capricious cell divisions on the way to reabsorption.


This, in the given moment, which is all we have and all we ever know, is exactly where black bag is supposed to be, and where they are. This is the day God wants me to live. I hope that doesn’t come across as sentimental.




The Longman Industrial Estate looks abandoned,
 
letters flaking off its billboard and list of Lots from 400 to 600. Brambles grow through the perimeter fence that encircles the entire complex. There is a thin wooden promontory where a gray, undecorated narrow boat with dark glass windows is moored. By pedaling furiously I am able to ground the swan craft on a small gravel beach that the gates open onto. I clamber out of the swan, sit on the gravel and close my eyes.


My phone has 6 percent battery remaining. I look at the map, which shows no roads or tracks leading to or from the estate. I suppose there was a road once, but aside from the city, the nearest town is seventeen miles away and probably isn’t much to write home about either. It would have a Wimpy, where you could get a burger on a plate with a knife and fork, and outside the Wimpy a man with a tracheotomy hole, smoking a king-size Mayfair through his tracheotomy hole. I could be him or his little dog whose fur is worn down like pigskin.


Tomorrow you will be with me in paradise.


My legs shake as I stand and I have to lean against the gate.


Within the complex I walk between squat gray buildings with sheet metal nailed over their windows, blasted wooden sheds and a barnlike structure covered in electric-green lichen. Halfway down the lot, I recognize the brick house with the garage door, and next to this, dominating the center of the wasteland, a large corrugated-metal warehouse with double doors slightly ajar.

82




I WATCH AS ANOTHER BLACK BAG
 
crunches through the gravel. My instinct is to hide behind a nearby shed, but then I realize that I look just like him and, if he’s noticed me, he is unfazed. He stumbles on an uneven concrete slab then calmly enters the building.


I follow him in.


From an unlit antechamber I can see through a flimsy glass-paneled door to a sort of hall with chairs arranged in rows and an aisle between, like a church.


I count the black bags. Nineteen … twenty. Most are sitting on padded wooden Low Church chairs. They face the front of the hall. The room is dimly lit with orange light, a moment of prayer in an empty art gallery. The smell of wood polish, sawdust and damp—a purposeful and comforting smell.


At the front is a slightly raised dais, a simple wooden podium with a mic stand but no microphone. There are twenty-seven black bags in total. They are identical. Similar in stature, and I can detect no difference in the design, tailoring, quality of leather or fit of black bag. There is no label in my black bag so I have no idea where they were designed or manufactured.


The ones who aren’t sitting stand around in twos and threes, as if in conversation, but not one of them is saying anything, I can detect not so much as a whisper, as if they have taken the vow of silence intrinsic to black bag and have gathered to honor it. There is something quite poignant about the sight of three black bags standing close together as if in conference, but in actuality only looking at one another and seeing only another black bag. Perhaps the glimmer of an eye through the little punched holes.


Everyone is barefoot. In the antechamber I quietly remove my flip-flops and kick them behind a cardboard box of fliers under a dark wooden table.


I feel self-conscious, hesitant as an interloper, and have to remind myself that here, more than anywhere else, I do not stand out at all. None of these black bags could possibly realize that I, black bag, am not supposed to be here. I enter the main room and take a seat by myself on the third row.


If someone is in charge and does a head count I could be in trouble, I suppose. But:


	1.	In a group of twenty-seven people on a given weekday evening, it is statistically inevitable that at least one might be sick, or held up in traffic, or has suffered some minor or major catastrophe that prevents their attendance. So it is likely that I, as black bag 28, will be taken as 28
 
of
 
28, or even 28 of 29, as opposed to a surplus black bag who has no right to be present.


	2.	That is if anyone
 
is
 
in charge and if, as the black bag in charge, one of their duties includes taking a silent anonymous head count, which, when I think it through, seems less and less likely, they might easily miscount anyway.


	3.	Nonetheless, should someone take a head count how would they identify an imposter out of twenty-eight identical black bags?


	4.	For all I know, and depending on what this meeting even is, it could well be that evangelism and eventual conversion is actively encouraged and therefore the presence of a new black bag would be cause for silent, heartfelt celebration.


I scan the black bags present. I wonder if the poor waif I accosted outside the supermarket is among them. I wonder if Claudio is among
 
them. I want to shout, Claudio? Is that you? But I’m worried that might constitute immediate grounds for my expulsion. I should remain silent until I get a feel for the place.


After some minutes the black bags who were standing in little groups move toward the rows until all twenty-eight of us are seated, facing silently forward. I feel my heart rate increase as if I am about to see my favorite band or hear the verdict from a jury of my peers. But nothing happens. I try not to draw attention to myself by moving, but I turn my head very slowly to check out the others. The black bags sit perfectly still and face the front. So I do the same. Five minutes.


I am not sure if the black bag who walks carefully down the aisle and takes the stage was one of our party all along or if he has entered the building only after the rest of us had assembled.


He climbs the dais.


He turns to face us.


He stands in front of us.


He stands before the podium and we look at him.


So much of life seems to come down to sitting in rows while another human being tells us something.




Brethren, he says.
 
We gather once more in a spirit of renunciation.


He has a slightly Scottish accent, his voice is not low or authoritative, but natural and unhurried. I am not sure if he is the leader of the black bags present or if it is simply his turn to speak.


Much has been said about atonement in the last few weeks, he says. But I want us not to be in any hurry. Atonement will take two, perhaps three generations. We seek, humbly, to start a process that will only bear fruit when we are gone. We’ll start with the recitation.


The other black bags have seemingly committed the creed to memory, but I find that I can just about keep up by finishing a syllable behind them and occasionally predicting the next word, like a charlatan in a choir, which is, I suppose, what I am.




I saw what I had become.


I 
saw what I had been.


I 
turned in shame.


I 
turned again.


I 
saw that everything I had was stolen.


I 
saw what I had done to distract myself.


I sat among my broken toys and wept.




This goes on for some time and I concentrate more on keeping up the charade than the content of the phrases themselves. Despite this, I think over my past acts of callousness or presumption. I think of Angelique and whether I have treated her fairly in my memory. I think of Justine, and wonder what she’s doing right now and what she’d make of a self-flagellating men’s group. I feel a little hungry and wish I’d thought to pack my pouch with almonds.


Thank you, says black bag. Now, as penitents, we may feel, we
 
must
 
feel, that there is no atonement. No savior is coming to reconcile us with the world.


I stand up.


I do not know why I do this.


It was involuntary, as if I’d suffered a small electric shock.


No savior is coming
, the black bag leader repeats, a little more steely this time.


I hear seagulls outside complaining of the sunset.


I sit down again.


Many are already lost, he continues. Many are demolished. We cannot concern ourselves with them anymore. There’s not enough time. We’ve started far too late.


The black bags begin to emit a low, bass drone. The more musically proficient black bags choose a note to harmonize. A couple of higher, undulating tones.


One day our sons, our friends’ sons, or the sons of our friends’ sons, will be well enough to step out of the bags. They will emerge healed, and whole, to take up their rightful place in society.


I do not like this at all.


But I am nothing if not obliging.


I join in with the drone.




It is almost nine when
 
we fall silent.


Someone clears their throat. Excuse me, he says, in a wispy RP. The boat will leave in five minutes. Please have your cards facing outward for scanning.


Cards?


I begin to sweat.


I could, at this point, shout for Claudio, just in case he’s one of the black bags. But I feel like that could get us both in trouble.


In the antechamber I find my flip-flops under the table and shuffle behind a long, tall cardboard box, where I sit down, hidden from the entrance. I must wait until every black bag has vacated the premises.


A smaller box is to my side. I turn and lift its flap: covered in polythene, a large folded leather black bag. A pile of them. I close my eyes.


I wait until I hear the low chug of the narrow boat’s engine.




Outside in the dark
 
I make my way across the compound to the cove, using my phone as a torch. This is not a public place. I kick the little jagged stones out of my flip-flops as I walk to the beached swan pedal boat. I feel the cold and the damp and I celebrate it.


The swan pedal boat rasps on the gravel as I nudge it back toward the water. I shiver, step in up to my ankles and push myself against its thick neck until it turns to face the opposite direction.

I AM EXHAUSTED AND BAD TEMPERED,
 
but my legs seem to have become conditioned to the constant pedaling.


I feel a little gross, the way a comedian might feel when people start shouting their catchphrase at them.


How like men to turn black bag into a secret society! Membership cards! Clandestine assemblies! This empty self-recrimination … it absolves you of nothing. God, they’ve got it all wrong from start to finish. They’ve found a way of making black bag about
 
them
, when the very point was to … Where to even start?


I lean to steer the swan away from the berm and pedal gently. I smell soil, the dank water, the tall weeds that have a kind of green and unnecessary stink just to let you know for sure they’re weeds.


The black bags went too far, did not go far enough. Do I want to be the only black bag?


I do if nobody else is doing it right.


It’s natural to feel a bit melancholy toward the end of a protracted run; wondering if your performance is still engaging or if it’s stagnated; natural also to realize that you may not have explored the full arc of your character and now it’s nearly too late.


The barge of self-loathing has cut so far ahead of me, I cannot even detect its wake—I am the only thing to disturb the waters.


What would Justine make of all this?


What would they make of Justine?


Those who take a vow of poverty or obedience or silence don’t do it because they
 
enjoy
 
it, per se.


Some things, however important, are almost too difficult to put into words—just as you can only fold a piece of paper, however large, in half
 
seven times before the radius of the curve becomes longer than the fold. It is a problem inherent to character; someone you made up can only make seven people up. Let’s paint a scene: a walled garden on a mild and sunny day. The warblers trill, the squirrels shake their fists … Here is our character. They might sit in a deck chair and read
 
The Trouble with Being Born
. This is a punch line—wherein both the ideas expressed by
 
The Trouble with Being Born
 
and the character themselves for reading it are mocked—and on such a beautiful day too. Outside this scene is a sensibility, an invisible adjudicator who genially invites us to stand in judgment with them. And thereby, as there is no place more comfortable than to stand in judgment, allows us to return, completely unperturbed by love or death, to the routines of everyday life. Our professional or recreational pursuits are really a form of displacement activity, up to seven layers deep, such that we have forgotten what we were originally trying to displace.




I have been pedaling for
 
almost ninety minutes when I see a car on an adjacent railway bridge that leads up from the towpath, its full beam headlights flashing, honking repeatedly.


Hey!


A man gets out of the car and stands on the bridge, waving his arms at me.


At first I want to get away from him as quickly as possible, but a swan pedal boat, as I have had ample chance to ascertain, can travel at a maximum of just-below-walking speed.


I try to steer into the bank, but the swan veers in the opposite direction.


Hey, over here!


Yes, I call, I can see you, I’m trying to—


He starts climbing down the stairs to the towpath.


When he gets closer I recognize him as Mason, still dressed in his sockless deck shoes and black linen trousers that don’t reach his ankles.


Mason! I say.


Yeah, hi, buddy. Jesus. Try to get a little closer—


I’m trying!


I pedal and lean hard and the swan arbitrarily drifts toward the right, where Mason is able to grab it by the neck and wrench it close to the crumbling brickwork at the murky water’s edge.


I haul myself out of the swan onto dry land and he helps me to my feet.


I double over, wheezing.


Are you okay? The fuck were you
 
doing
? he says.


The travails of the day are catching up with me and it takes me a while to get my breath.


Come on, bud. He takes black bag by the shoulder and guides me toward the steps. Let’s get you home.




Mason’s car is low, black and shapely,
 
without logos. I stand and shiver as the passenger door opens automatically. It feels like stepping into a lacquered jewelry box.


I’ll get the seat all wet.


The seat, says Mason, will dry you and warm you up.


The car makes a gentle orchestral sound as he starts it.


I feel as though I could fall asleep.


Oxford Road, right? he says.


Why did you come to get me? I murmur. What are you doing here?


You’re our most valuable asset, says Mason. You’re
 
the
 
black bag!


There are others, I say. I just saw them. How did you know where to—


Well, like, the map of course, he says, frowning. Like anyone can tell where you are all the time. You know that, right? I noticed you were throwing out some weird numbers into the chain and then I checked and I was all, Woah! Where in the hell is he? Is he in trouble?


I was getting pretty tired, I admit.


So I follow the line and I get to the bridge and it’s like the trail’s gone cold and I’m all, Ohhhhh, shit, he’s drowned.


I did almost drown earlier.


But then I see you in that fucking busted-up fairground boat and that’s when I started trying to get your attention. I mean don’t let me interfere with your process, but you
 
do
 
need to take care of yourself. I could have been in … Austin or somewhere.


Mason, I say, what is your involvement in black bag?


Huh? I figured Claudio would have told you.


He shoots me a querulous look.


You know this—I’m like your angel investor or whatever. You made a big impression on me so I looked it up and there you
 
were
, you dark horse. I got in touch with Claudio and he sent me the specs. I booked a little more time in the good ol’ US of K; spent days going around collecting bag tokens in AR, getting used to your scenes, your haunts; it’s good work—


I wasn’t involved in any of that, I say.


Naturally then I’m thinking,
 
What’s the application?
 
And then it hits me—


I haven’t seen Claudio in weeks, I interrupt. I was looking for him. That’s why I was … the swan.


That this is really about
 
love

Mason, do you know where he is? His phone’s dead. He hasn’t posted any new videos, even.

The car is so quiet I keep feeling surprised to be in motion.


I’ll look into it. I’ll look into it. I think he’s fine, he says. This is kind of a make-or-break moment and he’s probably tied up with meetings. That’s something we should be dispensing with altogether pretty soon: meetings. Fuck meetings!


Has he been in touch with you?


Hey, seriously, if anything was wrong, says Mason, I’d have heard about it.

You leave it with me. I’ll get hold of him and I’ll tell him to contact you as a matter of urgency, okay?


Okay, I say. Thanks, Mason.


I watch the nicer streets of the city blend into the low-rent district, the cash converters, the phone-case emporium. I think of telling Mason about the black bag meeting, but something holds my tongue. Instead I ask him about the app.

Oh—oh no.


Statistically inevitable. Mason shrugs. And naturally some bad faith motherfuckers are trying to spin it as a suicide but it’s not a suicide—it looks to me like an accident, and the guy had substance abuse issues anyway.


Right.


Not to be callous.


No.


So now behind several layers of security there’s an imaginary adult-aged kid who can’t stop monologuing about the day their father died and how it screwed up their life.

The pandect?


When you got that
 
aerial view
, when you already know how someone’s life is going to play out with a teeny-tiny margin of error. It could make you cold-hearted. It does. Whenever you zoom in, you realize you’re watching a little shrew, just a half-crazy little shrew trying to make its way across a big open field with all the owls and falcons and shit. What do you Brits say?
 
You’re playing a blinder.

Americans have a peculiar talent for sounding consoling and threatening at the same time. I quite enjoy it.

I WAKE UP WITH ALL
 
of my limbs on fire, having repeatedly used muscles I have never so much as flexed before in piloting the swan. I check my phone. It’s gone ten. Ugh. I stagger to my feet.


Justine has messaged me on One-Way during the night:


I am texting you because we haven’t broken up and I expect you’ll have realized this by the morning.


Justine, I write back, I’m sorry to ask a favor but could you possibly find Dr. Blend’s home address for me?


A pause.


You think because of my discipline I can just instantly access people’s personal data?! she replies.


This is followed, in less than ten seconds and before I can apologize, by an image of Dr. Blend’s home address, telephone number, personal and work emails, date of birth and religion (None).


Very presumptuous, Justine adds.


I look at black bag, draped dejectedly over the end of my bed.


Come on then, I say.




You never know what to expect
 
of someone’s home, a sight more intimate than the naked body as it includes our every thought and necessity made visible. Most of our instincts derive from trying, preemptively, to avoid disappointment.


Sometimes people with a rich inner life live in pristine nineties new builds with no art and bright lights because they simply do not see the point in anything other than their discipline, but lack the conviction of a Corbusier—which would be too close to an aesthetic—they are, after all, a product of their time and nationality by accident of birth.
 
Suburban wastelands that required a new kind of antidepressant, pale orange houses with blank expressions and consistent plastic windows. In such a home a single flower stem in a thin glass tube could make you scream. At its hideous reminder of life.


On the other hand, sometimes intellectuals live in a state of
 
actual decay
 
in damp cottages surrounded by snails, grates overflowing with ashes, something more permanent than them growing between their teeth, swaying heaps of books to the ceiling, spines inward, for some reason, even on the shelves. Their system of order exclusively whimsical; implements mixed on every surface—bellows, used cooking spatula, inkwell—because they had to stir a stew before dribbling it down their shirt, and tending the fire, and then had an idea. They sit in the middle like a great, dusty mushroom, thinking deep thoughts. Holy fools, homeowners, with the right to choose exactly how revoltingly they live in pursuit of higher things.


In the event, Blend’s house, 44 Pilgrim’s Moorings, is a postwar semidetached with a long garden for growing vegetables and black currants during rationing. I have to duck the branches of a cooking-apple tree to reach the front door.


I feel like a small boy who has clambered through a gap in the park fence to emerge into a complete stranger’s life for no reason.


I press the sing-song, English Protestant kind of doorbell.


I can hear the vague histrionics of a minor opera on a radio.


Blend appears, at first a peach-colored form behind the frosted glass, then he opens the door and sees me. I bob up and down a bit.


Oh, he says, too surprised to sound worried by my presence.


He looks tired, peering out from the folds around his eyes, which seem to have grown more pronounced and heavy, sloping down to the itchy white hairs of his beard. Perhaps he’s been drinking. He wears a blue fisherman’s jumper and gray corduroy trousers.


I hope this is okay.


Yes. He puts his hands to the bones above his eyes and massages them. I suppose you might as well come in.


The foyer of Dr. Blend’s house is tidy and well kept—a bureau desk, a standing lamp. There is a framed block print of Dionysus drinking from a bowl of wine.


I can see no evidence of a current or former partner, photos of grown-up children, suggestions of combined preference or compromise, but even the formulation that runs through my head—
I can see no evidence—
annoys me. I also see no dog chews or scratching posts. Isn’t it, in fact, the height of rudeness—and isn’t rudeness just the dull, persistent, everyday manifestation of cruelty—to be curious about anyone else’s personal arrangements that they’ve not actively volunteered to you? To be always trying to
 
work people out
 
like a dismal magazine journalist. Lord spare us all from such barbarism. There is a good and a bad curiosity to entertain about the lives of others, and time and again it feels like we’ve chosen the bad. Black bag will draw a close to this entire way of life.


There is a canvas director’s chair in the alcove by the staircase and a slim-legged table with a cordless landline phone in its charging cradle. Next to the phone, a black square plate holds several white fingernail crescents and a shiny pair of clippers.


Dr. Blend moves a floppy phone directory over the plate.


I feel embarrassed, but this kind of thing happens when you turn up unannounced, and I take it as a good sign that a jobless Blend is still making an effort about his personal appearance and hygiene.


I have always found clipping my fingernails deeply unpleasant, he says. It feels like my flesh being cut. Either, in timidity, I don’t go far enough and might as well not have bothered, or I cut to the quick and it stings for days. But … one must cut one’s fingernails.


It’s true, I agree.


He picks up some letters, shuffles them and puts them on the bureau.


I don’t often have visitors, he mutters.


It’s a lovely house! I say, senselessly.




Blend arranges us in armchairs
 
on either side of a tea table, facing the mantelpiece of an immaculate open fireplace, the window to our
 
left overlooking the back garden. He has poured two cups of Earl Grey with lemon.


I hope this doesn’t seem forward, I say. Coming to your house.


No, I ought to have contacted you, he says. Remiss of me. Then he frowns. For goodness’ sake, you
 
do
 
realize you don’t have to wear that anymore?


Do you want me to return it? I ask, trying not to sound panicked.


No … No, you may dispose of it as you wish.


I’ve become quite attached to it.


Dr. Blend grimaces.


Is this how you tend to behave when a play finishes its run?


Oh, haha, no of course not, I say, can you imagine?


And how is this different?


It is natural that Blend might use the same Socratic method on me as he would on one of his students, so I take the opportunity to consider his question. I look out the window. The back garden is something of a disaster: an excessive incline to the rear fence that might once have been grass but is now mostly mud, flints, tentative stalks of bramble from the wasteland beyond, scouting the area and finding it much to their satisfaction.


Has
 
the play finished its run? I say.


Blend picks up his cup of tea, blows on it and puts it down.


Wait there, he says.


He rises and leaves the room and I hear him crashing around in the next room. Presently he comes back with a pile of documents. He puts the stack on his chair, takes the first page and holds it up.


A black-and-white photograph of a fairly good-looking young man in the top right corner. His name, address, phone number and email, and details of his qualifications and past roles on stage and screen.


Dr. Blend drops the actor’s CV and shows me the next.


A black-and-white photograph of a fairly good-looking young man in the top right corner. His name, address, phone number and email, and details of his qualifications and past roles on stage and screen.


What are these?


Your forerunners, he says. I’ve been replicating the black bag experiment on this module for the last … thirty years. It was a practical exercise, a real-world experience for the class—the classes, I should add, were at one point quite small … But something always went wrong. The men would talk to the other students after class, break character, one of them couldn’t stop laughing—that was extremely annoying. One told the students everything about the experiment after the first session—can you believe that?


The lack of professionalism astounds me, I say.


Another, in the late nineties, ended up having sexual relationships with half the cohort. Each time pretending it was his first.
 
You’re the only person who’s ever got me out of the bag.
 
You understand?


God, I say.


That took a lot of unpacking.


A different era, the nineties.


But then I noticed something else, later, say around 2009 onward.


After the financial crash! I say.


I’m not sure what that has to do with anything, says Dr. Blend.


I was pretty good? I say. In comparison?


From 2009 the black bags began to disappear in the final week of the experiment, Blend says. They didn’t show for the last session. The students were rather saddened by their absence.


Thus proving the … hypothesis? I offer.


Once, says Blend, I thought I saw one of them, still as black bag, mooching around outside the Physics Department, but it’s possible I imagined it.


No, I tell him, I don’t think you imagined it. I think many of them are living as black bag permanently.


Why on
 
earth
 
would they do that? says Blend.


I think they’ve set up some kind of movement.


In any case, you were my last roll of the dice. I’ve been resisting my impending retirement for several years now because I still felt like I could make a difference. A university can make it very clear when it wants to get rid of you and replace you with someone more efficient for
 
half your salary. If I seemed distracted this term it was because I’d likely just come from shouting at my younger superiors in “tribunal” meetings.




A wall-mounted black-and-white flip clock
 
displays the correct time of 12:02, but Dr. Blend’s sun-damaged calendar, April’s image a print of William Morris’s
 
Kelmscott Chaucer
, is from 2013. I get an uneasy, prickly feeling from this—have I fallen through some kind of temporal anomaly so that all of my interactions with Dr. Blend are taking place over a decade in the past? Is anyone so parsimonious as to keep the same paper calendar for ten years or more?


Nobody knows what to make of the twenty-first century. On the mantelpiece, next to a dusty white candle: a porcelain swan, a small matchbox double-decker bus, a wooden narrow boat.


Poor Dr. Blend.


You held out because you felt you could make a difference, I say, to what?


This is a strange job, he says. We’re supposedly the finest minds in our respective disciplines, but we’re required to spend most of our time boring hungover teenagers to sleep.


I don’t know.


You meet people, by and large, at a formative time in their lives, a very
 
impressionable
 
age. That’s a responsibility in itself.


Has there ever been an era more malign in its ideologies? says Blend. More absent a coherent antithesis?


The Crusades?


Oblivious hedonism, fundamentalist religion, evolutionary psychology, says Blend. A dunderheaded and unexamined combination of them all until it becomes the natural element.


Yes! I say.


I want them to look beyond the case studies and embrace the fact that
 
we
 
are the experiment. You know … well, you don’t, that’s why I’m telling you: Retirement feels rather similar to being expelled from a cult, Blend says. Everything you measured your life by is proved to be utterly
 
senseless and haphazard. You went along with a set of precepts because you had little choice in the matter once you’d signed up, and then it’s suddenly over and the prevailing feeling is Well what was all that in aid of? It’s curious.


But you can still write? I say. You can still do your research, even if nobody’s making you do it. We can get used to anything—isn’t that what the black bag experiment teaches us?


Blend stares at the empty fireplace.


Isn’t it?


I thought I could help people find meaning in their lives, he says, softly.


I’m sure your students remember you very fondly, I gush.


But I think that meaning … I think instead, I presided over the last ebbing away of enchantment, pointing out the occasional shiny thing as it flowed into the drain. I wonder if I wasn’t just tricking them into feeling more comfortable with the coming meaninglessness. Plumping the coffin pillows.


This is when I usually open a bottle of wine, I say. When I start thinking like that.


All the while dedicating my entire life to a system that was doing the same thing to me.


There’s a reason booze is popular. I appreciate you seeing me again, I say. I do. It’s nice to debrief. Could you tell me one last thing?


Blend raises his eyebrows in assent.


Why the obsession? Why did you rerun the same experiment over and over again?


We listen to his wall clock ticking.


In general, we live by comparison and competition, says Blend, after a pause. You’d be depressed to learn how many findings from psychological research are applied to concepts in business school to manipulate consumer habits. Brand awareness, marketing, models of leadership, the constant slurry of consumer economics. The original experiment probably contributed to the making of several fortunes in adhesives, detergents, vacuum cleaners … But black bag isn’t trying to sell you anything.


Ah, I say.


Black bag isn’t trying to convince you of anything, or influence you in any way. With black bag there is no comparison, no competition. It is a confrontation with something …


With what?


…


Dr. Blend?

I ARRANGE SOME DRIED FLOWERS
 
in a red apothecary vase on my parents’ windowsill.


There.


I smile at my father, still handsome in a rugged, stony menhir sort of a way. I place my hand on my mother’s shoulder.


That’s better, isn’t it?




In the half room on
 
the way to the toilet, which is used, first and foremost, to house the vacuum cleaner in the manner to which it has grown accustomed, there is a large gold-framed photograph on the wall.


I am not used to seeing much on my parents’ walls—they tend to value a clean, uninterrupted wall.


The photograph is of
 
me
 
as Ariel in my school’s production of
 
The Tempest
.


I was sixteen. In the photograph I am wearing a pair of pants made out of white leaves and my hair has been waxed into fine tapered tendrils, reaching to infinity. My false eyebrows are long antennae. My body is painted with patches of silver glitter and I am posed against a black background, knees bent and one hand spread across my chest—I gaze up and to the right as if in appreciation of some wonder just out of shot.


I look androgynous and otherworldly.


God, I really was good. Not that it makes me special. Plenty of people are good. Hardly anyone gets what they deserve, for better
 
or
 
worse.


I look at the photo a little longer.


No, that’s not true. I
 
am
 
special. No sense being modest about it. If we overvalue art and abuse the power it vests in us, it’s because it’s special.


In that moment I realized
, I tell the future journalist in my head,
 
that this was ultimately the only reason I’m on the planet. It was a turning point.


Ariel, it is worth remembering, does not actually have many lines in
 
The Tempest
. One of them is “No.” One of them is “No, sir.” Another is “I do not, sir.”


Oh, you
 
guys
, I say out loud.


I take a finger to my misty eyes.


At my age I would like to be something of a reassurance to them, my parents, would like to make it clear that I have, come what may, things under control for them. The means and the ways.


Fuck’s sake.


You’ll start to get ill and I’ll be completely useless. I could pay for about … half an hour of basic care.


I would like to be able to look after you, but I can’t because I have stubbornly committed myself to an art and vocation with no future and precious little present, rather than training as an accountant or a stockbroker or maybe a lawyer. And out of what? Sheer perversity of spirit, encouragement at an impressionable moment (the nineties), and, by this point, sunk cost fallacy.


Isn’t it time someone rescued me from the cult of myself for deprogramming?


To trust in the arts is an act of hubris so self-destructive the gods don’t even bother to punish you for it. It is to become a monstrous politician of an insecure republic, an estate agent of your own soul, a demon of lubricity.


There is just a chance, though, the width of a crack in a martini glass, that we need it more than ever.




I glance to the hallway
 
and can see that the two pillars of rock are now casting a shadow in their bedroom, preparing for a nap.


What I want to say is thank you. English people have such a deep-seated suspicion of disingenuousness that we often forego gratitude
 
altogether. It is feasible to go your entire life without ever expressing a single emotion just in case you didn’t mean it.


I forget who people are, just as I forget who or what myself is. I forget, when I talk to you, that you are my mother, I forget, when I talk to you, that you are my father. To remember it would feel like looking directly at the sun. We have to forget, almost constantly, that we love
 
anyone
 
or life would be intolerable.


I just desperately want you all to be okay.


You included.


If God would countenance so vague a prayer.


I ask that sometimes the voice in your head—the one that weaves and pulls and frays without ceasing—runs out of threads to tangle together and says,


You know what? Go and amuse yourself for a while.


And you look around, bemused for a moment, not sure whether to take it seriously, whether you can trust it at all after everything it’s put you through … And then you run off in a black bag like a child and never look back.




OceanofPDF.com

















86




WELL, SLAY THE FATTED CALF,
 
says Elliott, when he sees me coming down the stairs, still sniffling a little.


This has nothing to do with our conversation last time, I tell him. I’m still black bag. I just needed a little break.


I walk heavily, staring at the ground. When we reach the stile by the twisted tree I stop and can barely summon the energy to climb it.


What is it?
 
says Elliott.
 
You seem more than usually put upon
.


Claudio, I say.


Claudio
?


I don’t know where he is. I think he’s got involved with some weird movement.


There’s a lesson there, isn’t there
? growls Elliott.
 
That you are quite happy abasing your
self
, but the moment someone else does it, you object.


We’re probably all a bit like that, I tell him.


My own heart let me more have pity on
, he says
.


I didn’t know you liked poetry, Elliott.


Only Hopkins
, he snarls.


I think you’re going a little soft, aren’t you?


Thyself thy foe, to thy sweet self too cruel.


All right, all right.


Elliott pounces into a pile of goosegrass and emerges seconds later, returns to my side.


Thought I saw something
, he says.
 
Would you ever kill a man
?


That’s more like it, I say. No.


What if he was trying to kill someone you loved
?


In that case, I suppose yes, certainly.


Good
, says Elliott.
 
For love is bloody and needs must be. And if he was trying to kill
 
you?


I pause.


So you see that this is a failure of love
, says Elliott.
 
You are lacking in love. Now give me a gravy bone.


I left them behind.


Elliott stares at me, dismayed.


Wait, there’s a strip of jerky in my pocket.


I toss it to him and he catches it in midair.


Good boy.


We keep walking through the light mist.


If it horrifies you that your friend would reduce himself to a silent, anonymous blob
, he says,
 
you might extend the same grace to yourself, that’s all.




OceanofPDF.com

















87




I AM EATING MY LAST
 
square of dark chocolate, watching a video essay about self-actualization and waiting for word from Mason. I don’t know if I would describe Mason as trustworthy, exactly, but has he not been quite direct and candid with me during our brief acquaintance? If he is untrustworthy he is openly so, and would probably tell me if he’d pushed Claudio in front of a train after share prices collapsed. That’s something people do, when there’s money involved, isn’t it? Very little is off the table. If we hate the rich we should also feel very sorry for them. In their humiliation, because as a flower of the field they will pass away.


Instead I receive an email from strange-acting-jobs. I have landed neither the breath mints, nor the open-top-bus music video, nor the theater-in-education play about consent, which I applied for but was too embarrassed to tell you about. I have, however, been chosen by Neural Plural for their continuing mission to understand and fully capture in gross, gross detail how human beings walk, speak, move and think. Good for me.


The doorbell rings. I am not accustomed to receiving visitors at this hour.


I finish my square of chocolate, wedge the door to my flat open with its cardboard packet and head for the porch.


Leaning in the doorway, worn out and unshaven, his hair more unkempt than usual, is Claudio. I do not know whether to kiss him or take him by the shoulders and shake him.


All right? he says.




Okay, says Claudio, seated next to me
 
with a pint glass of port. Some crazy shit. I start seeing these other black bags—


Yes, I watched your stream. I’ve seen them too!


Yeah—just wandering around, like what the hell—I don’t know if my followers are just getting really into it or
 
what
 
it is, but I stake one of them out and eventually he leads me—


The hall, the meeting, I’ve been there, I say. I traced it from your video.


You “traced” it, says Claudio, listen to him! No that’s great, so you’ve seen it.


I went to a service.


Wild, right? says Claudio. It’s been going for years, it turns out. So I’m at the meeting.


How did you get there?


On a barge.


How did you get your own bag?


Claudio looks at the floor.


I’m not proud of this, he says. I stole it.


You stole it from where?


Ugh, says Claudio. I followed one of the black bags, cornered him in an alleyway and overpowered him.


This he reports with some distaste.


I got him in a headlock and I stole his black bag and left him in his pants in the middle of the street.


Claudio! Have we, after all this, learned
 
nothing
?


He was really upset and I felt bad. I apologized. I threw him my jumper.


Terrible.


Yeah, I know.


Just terrible.


But I’m at the meeting, says Claudio, blah blah blah, chant chant chant, and afterward I collar the guy, the head of the chapter.


The Scottish guy?


Scottish black bag who seems to be the ringleader and I say, Hey. I unzip my bag and stick my head out so he can see my face—he’s all like, NO! covering his eyeholes. I’m like, Hey, what is this? I’ve got exclusive
 
rights to black bag including points on merchandising and you’re all in breach of copyright. And then it’s like he recognizes me.
 
You
, he says. I’m like, Yeah, it’s me, Claudio, “Don’t forget to like and subscribe.” He says, We’ve been looking for you. Oh. yeah? I say. You can’t have been trying very hard if I found you first. Now, at this point a bunch of the other black bags have gathered around me. I mean it’s not necessarily threatening, but, you know. I’m a little nervous.


I point out to Claudio that they may have heard that he beat one of them up.


Right. Though then the Scottish bag says I’m free to go, right now, they’ll even give me a lift back, but I have to give up my projects, I have to relinquish any ownership of black bag as a concept, to which I do
 
not
 
have any rights, exclusive or otherwise, and I’m like, um, no.
 
Or
, he says, he can bring me in.


Bring you in?


Bring me in and we can work things out. Saco a saco.


Okay. Saco a saco.


I have to give up my phone. They assign me a chaperone. Once I’m off the boat, they bundle me into a car and they get me on the next flight to Copenhagen—


Copenhagen?


Yeah, to the Center for Advanced Studies in Masculinity. This place is insane, dude. It’s like a cathedral, but a new-build cathedral. They live in simple little rooms; they eat olives and bread from a little pouch in the bags. Men in black bags are milling around not saying anything to each other, not judging each other, just humbly getting on with maintaining the Center for Advanced Studies in Masculinity.


No River of Shame.


No River of Shame! I get kind of into the lifestyle in all honesty; it’s a real detox. I have to have these meetings every day with the superior black bags—I don’t know how they can tell who’s superior, who’s an elder, maybe its random. Maybe it’s leg hair. But these black bags, they take me to a little room, all knowing like, Oh, of course, Number 278—that’s my number—we have
 
much
 
to discuss. But really they didn’t have
 
very much to discuss, they just wanted to know what I was doing, what my interest was in the movement. And when I’m not being questioned they pretty much left me to my own devices.


You couldn’t have got word to me?


Every day I thought I was on my way back, says Claudio. Then they’d keep me for another night. I’m sorry.


I was getting really worried.


An oversight, says Claudio. Shouldn’t have happened.


What exactly are they working on at the Center for Advanced Studies in Masculinity?


Well this is it, says Claudio. After a few days away from my phone, I had an epiphany: Men are mostly frightened and crazy and very easily misled. They just want someone to be nice to them, but they don’t know how to ask for that.


Hmm.


The thing with privilege, says Claudio, is not to take it personally. If you have something other people don’t … It’s probably something other people
 
should
 
have—a safe and stable place to live, smaller class sizes, access to potable water, an opportunity to succeed in your vocation, love, art … It’s not that you’re
 
bad
 
for having those things; anyone other than a monster would want those things to be universal. I think you can acknowledge that without getting defensive.


Right. So you’re just—


When you get defensive you start making an implausible case for why you deserve it. You worked harder, you suffered more. Bullshit. It’s evil. Yeah, so I’m just living that bag life for days on end. Ultimately, in exchange for a four-point-five percent cut of profits, we’re allowed to carry on.


What?


They felt it could be mutually beneficial, Bag Coin, all that shit, earn some losers a little travel money, attract some more men to their cause. Mason’s dealing with the legal side, but you know, he owns a controlling stake now, anyway.


You didn’t tell me anything about Mason.


Look, one: You told me you didn’t want to know what I was doing, and two: This was all happening at pretty much the same time, says Claudio. That’s how this stuff goes.


I scowl into the corner and then take a long sip of my port.


Isn’t it quite exploitative, Claudio, I say, to use your platform, by which I mean some hundreds of thousands of people with whom you have built up a level of trust over many years, in order to sell a … hokey new digital currency? And I mean quite as in completely.


Babe. Those guys aren’t as witless and vulnerable as they pretend to be, says Claudio. Something I learned over the last couple of weeks is that on some level we
 
want
 
to be conned.


No we don’t.


Like you with what’s her name … Angelique.


I bristle.


What about her?


On some level you sought out a dystopian relationship—


Dysfunctional
 
relationship.


Completely dysfunctional, agrees Claudio. Same with that Justine. You look for relationships like that because … I mean I’m not a shrink. But you’re trying to work something out.


What am I trying to work out?


You let people walk all over you!


They shouldn’t want to!


But they do! You get someone to treat you quite badly and then you’re all,
 
Why does this keep happening to me?


I punch him in the shoulder, which is quite solid.


I don’t think that’s how I sound at all. Have you been lifting?


Yeah, I want nice arms. But the point is, says Claudio, with something like Bag Coin, the exploitation is collaborative. Like in your pantomime. We all have to close our eyes very tight and say that we believe in fairies so that the whole operation doesn’t die. Create a deep faith in the assets. Ultimately, you and me …


You and me what?


We’re looking at several hundred grand each, says Claudio, which I know isn’t insane, but I trust Mason and there’ll probably be a regular monthly income on top of that. You’re probably not going to have to work anymore, that’s all.


I think about this while Claudio drinks his port. Don’t lottery winners mostly go batshit and kill themselves?


I mean that’s great, I say. I don’t know if it …


You can do whatever you want! says Claudio, Open a theater!


Claudio, opening a theater could bankrupt a small country. There’s a reason the whole patron of the arts thing kind of died out. Overheads!


A theater company, then. You’re always saying you want actual plays, written by actual writers, for actual actors to act in. You can make a space for that.


Oh.


You’re always saying our entire country has committed cultural suicide by mutual agreement.


I do say that.


So now you can do something about it! I just want you to be free to pursue what you really love, says Claudio. All of the issues with elitism in the arts we hear so much about—I want you to have some of that elitism.


That’s really sweet, I tell him.


And I’ll get back to keeping my people happy. I need to put a few more days into
 
Dados Mania
.


They’re going
 
nuts
, I say. Conspiracy theories out the wazoo.


Yeah, yeah, says Claudio. I’ve already announced I’m dropping a video tonight. Told them I got kidnapped. Released a new series of NFTs I drew on the plane. They’re fine. Mason says the whole kidnapping angle is actually the best thing that could have happened for the brand.


I get up and pace from one end of the room to the other, pausing to adjust my curtains.


What’s wrong? says Claudio. You seem worried.


It’s just … I say. I don’t really understand why you’d do this for me.


Because, says Claudio, I love you.


Oh, I say.


I walk back to the sofa and sit down.


I love you too, I guess.


You’d better. This place smells very strongly of damp, says Claudio. You ever get that checked out?


It’s vintage.


Claudio leans into me and I relax against him.


You free for lunch tomorrow?

88




I STAND IN LINE FOR
 
a coffee at a campus concession, black bag folded into a shopping bag at my side. Soon I will find a quiet bathroom and change.


The queue is long and slow, but I turned up early and spent some time in the library, as myself, and I read, on a whim, some Strindberg. This is something I will have to get used to.


I am going to be, by my own standards, very rich.


I’m not quite sure what this will do to me or my motivation.


I suppose I worry that there will be nothing left of my personality at all.


Have you ever met a man who got exactly what he wanted? They always seem a little confused, even shell-shocked. They speak at one remove, as if delivering their own eulogy.


I wish to be alive, to continue to live, even should that mean a state of constant deferral. How much do you want to appear in other people’s thoughts at all? As little as possible, I think. If I could, I would take everything I have ever thought about anyone else and delete it forever.


I shuffle forward.


Our only obligation to each other should be a vast, oceanic affection. Our only assumption must be that everybody else’s life is more meaningful than our own. That is the only way to encounter the other. The rest is window dressing.


I hear the barista call the name Justine.


I watch Professor Justine Pearce collect her flat white from the service counter as I step forward in the queue. It takes me a moment to recognize her because Justine has changed her hair to a short bob, which she has dyed blonde. She is wearing a small black blazer with a two-inch strip of hessian pinned to the lapel.


Our eyes meet. She narrows hers a little.


Can I help you? she says.


No, I’m sorry, I say. I’m a big fan of your work, that’s all.


Oh, Justine says. Well, thank you.




OceanofPDF.com

















89




WEEK TEN IN THE ACADEMIC CALENDAR.
 
The number of completion.


I am back in the regular lecture theater for
 
Attraction Theory—Principles of Social Psychology
. I sit four rows from the back, three seats in from the left. The lights flash fifteen times a second, the standard sad kitchen fluorescent that torments houseflies, but each is encased in a sculpted plastic scabbard that curves handsomely. Good luck, they seem to say, like a cat darting out between two walls, looking at you, then darting back again, Good luck trying to be a human being.


Everyone is here. The Sophies, the Toms, the Daniels, the girl who smells of shoe polish. Many others I wish I could tell you about. But my time is short.


Dr. Mazower is already at the front as the students file in.


He looks up, takes off his spectacles to rub them, puts them back on again.


The hypothesis was offered that repeated exposure of the individual to a stimulus object, he says, increases positive feelings toward said object. The best way to understand this is to experience it for ourselves. We might want to think about
 
why
 
this is the case. Firstly the reduction of uncertainty: we dislike uncertainty and we become less uncertain about something if we’re familiar with it.


The students either scratch down a note or clack on their laptops.


Secondly, says Mazower, ease of interpretation. Think of a film with a very complicated plot. Think of the second time you watch it and how much more you understand and appreciate it. We could also think of the repetitions in religious services and rituals, the way they make familiar shapes out of life, the way we develop habits and then define ourselves through them. There are several repercussions of this experience,
 
the mere-exposure effect—it could be used against us, for example. It would be sad, wouldn’t it, if the greatest breakthroughs in psychology of the twentieth century had largely been turned toward advertising and population control? In fact, I’d like to conclude the module with some thoughts about what the effect we’ve been studying exposes—no pun intended—and how we might learn from it.


Mazower pauses.


But before that I would like to introduce our visiting lecturer for the final week.


The fire escape at stage right opens and in walks Dr. Blend, wearing full academic robes. A black gown and hood with embroidered green trimming.


This excites the room into a collective whisper.


I sit up very straight. I remember my directions. I will not make any movements that denote an emotional response.


I’m happy to invite Dr. Blend, says Mazower, before his retirement commences at the end of this session, to share some concluding statements.


Please, hush, Blend says as he takes the lectern. Thank you.


I would like … Dr. Blend stops. He seems a little choked up. There are no seminars this week. Maybe he is always like this when it comes time to say goodbye.


I would like, before we sadly wrap up for the semester, to invite someone else to the stage. I feel it might be appropriate, at long last, to show our appreciation for his hard work over the last few months.


After a pause I realize that Dr. Blend is staring at
 
me
 
with a warm smile, his eyebrows raised.


Oh dear god, no, I mutter.


The boy next to me gives me a friendly nudge on the shoulder.


I get to my feet, pulling black bag straight, and the faces in the room swivel to follow Dr. Blend’s gaze. Without exception they look at me with expressions of such joy and adulation that I would not feel worthy of it had I sacrificed my life for them. Had I barricaded the door to prevent
 
an active shooter from taking anyone’s life but mine, and this a little treat from God in the afterlife. Even then I would cringe. Ach, it was nothing really.


Three students get out of my way so that I can exit the row, and their standing provokes a wave of other students to stand, and to break into applause. They clap with such intensity—somebody whoops, which inspires further whooping and cheering—and I stumble, my feet too close together due to the hem of black bag, awkwardly down the broad steps to the front of the lecture theater. I feel as though I am walking down an escalator running upward.


I scan the crowd. They’re really going for it. I can see the second Sophie, in row H, surrounded by her friends, clapping with her arms over her head, her smile a little indulgent but all the more sincere for that. As I clamber onto the stage and approach the podium a beaming Dr. Blend steps back to let me … bask, I suppose. I suppose I ought to bask. Is this not everything you ever wanted? Then why, I wonder, does it feel so—

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS




The original black bag experiment was conducted by Charles Goetzinger in 1967 at Oregon State University in order to test Robert Zajonc’s mere-exposure effect. He observed that, over the course of the semester, “the students’ attitude changed from hostility toward the Black Bag to curiosity and finally to friendship” (Goetzinger, quoted in Zajonc’s paper, “Attitudinal Effects of Mere Exposure”). In happening across this while researching a poem, I was immediately (which is to say obsessively and exclusively) concerned with black bag himself.


I am very grateful to Holly Pester for constant support, discussion, and a keener intelligence than my own. To Ruth Gilligan for the handbag. To Ben Pester for generously exchanging manuscripts at a critical moment. To Luke Brown for his customary precision and insight. Thank you, so much, to Georgia Garrett and Ivy Pottinger-Glass at RCW; to Nicholas Pearson at John Murray; and Caolinn Douglas at Zando. The kind of editors one dreams of.


The legalistic convention of beginning each element in a list paragraph with the word
 
Item
 
in italics followed by a full stop comes from somewhere, I think a nineteenth-century novel in translation, but I’ve been looking for it for the last year and have completely failed to track it down. I was reading a lot of Strindberg’s diaries and notebooks at the time of writing, and it’s possible it came from there (although I can’t find it).

ABOUT THE AUTHOR




Luke Kennard
 
is an award-winning poet and novelist from the United Kingdom. His collection
 
Cain
 
was shortlisted for the International Dylan Thomas Prize in 2016, and
 
Notes on the Sonnets
 
won the Forward Prize for Best Poetry Collection in 2021.
 
The Transition
, his first novel, was a BBC Radio 4 Book at Bedtime and longlisted for the Desmond Elliott Prize.
 
The Book of Jonah
, his seventh poetry collection, was published in 2025. He lives in the West Midlands, where he teaches creative writing at the University of Birmingham.
